***Tyrannosaurus*** ([/tɪˌrænəˈsɔːrəs, taɪ-/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English))[^cite_note-title-1] is a [genus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genus) of large [theropod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theropoda) dinosaur. The [type species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_species) ***Tyrannosaurus rex*** (*rex* meaning 'king' in [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin)), often shortened to ***T. rex*** or colloquially **T-Rex**, is one of the best represented theropods. It lived throughout what is now western [North America](21139.md), on what was then an island continent known as [Laramidia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laramidia). *Tyrannosaurus* had a much wider range than other [tyrannosaurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosauridae). [Fossils](10958.md) are found in a variety of [geological formations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geological_formations) which have been [dated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geochronology) to the late [Maastrichtian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maastrichtian) age of the late [Cretaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous) period, 69 to 66 [million years ago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mya_\(unit\)), with isolated specimens possibly indicating an earlier origin in the middle [Campanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanian) age. It was one of the last known members of the tyrannosaurids and among the last non-[avian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird) dinosaurs to exist before the [Cretaceous–Paleogene extinction event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous%E2%80%93Paleogene_extinction_event).

Like other tyrannosaurids, *Tyrannosaurus* was a [bipedal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biped) carnivore with a massive skull balanced by a long, heavy tail. Relative to its large and powerful hind limbs, the forelimbs of *Tyrannosaurus* were short but unusually powerful for their size, and they had two clawed digits. The most complete specimen measures 12.3–12.4 m (40–41 ft) in length, but according to most modern estimates, *Tyrannosaurus* could have exceeded sizes of 13 m (43 ft) in length, 3.7–4 m (12–13 ft) in hip height, and 8.8 t (8.7 long tons; 9.7 short tons) in mass. Although some other theropods might have rivaled or exceeded *Tyrannosaurus* in [size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_size), it is still among the largest known land predators, with its estimated bite force being the largest among all terrestrial animals. By far the largest carnivore in its environment, *Tyrannosaurus rex* was most likely an [apex predator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apex_predator), preying upon [hadrosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrosaur), juvenile armored herbivores like [ceratopsians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratopsia) and [ankylosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankylosaur), and possibly [sauropods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauropod). The question of whether *Tyrannosaurus* was an apex predator or a pure scavenger was among the longest debates in [paleontology](23084.md). Most paleontologists today accept that *Tyrannosaurus* was a predator, and the idea of *Tyrannosaurus* being primarily a scavenger has been discredited.[^cite_note-2]

Some [specimens of *Tyrannosaurus rex*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Specimens_of_Tyrannosaurus) are nearly complete skeletons. [Soft tissue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_tissue) and [proteins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protein) have been reported in at least one of these specimens. The abundance of fossil material has allowed significant research into many aspects of the animal's biology, including its life history and [biomechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomechanics). The feeding habits, [physiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiology), and potential speed of *Tyrannosaurus rex* are a few subjects of debate. Its [taxonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxonomy_\(biology\)) is also controversial. The Asian *[Tarbosaurus bataar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarbosaurus_bataar)* is very closely related to *Tyrannosaurus* and has sometimes been seen as a species of this genus. Several North American tyrannosaurids have been [synonymized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synonym_\(biology\)) with *Tyrannosaurus*, while some *Tyrannosaurus* specimens have been proposed as distinct species. The validity of these species, such as the more recently discovered ***T. mcraeensis***, is contentious.

*Tyrannosaurus* has been one of the best-known dinosaurs since the early 20th century. Its fossils have been a popular attraction in museums and it has appeared in media like *[Jurassic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park)*.

## History of research

See also: [Specimens of *Tyrannosaurus*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Specimens_of_Tyrannosaurus)

### Earliest finds



[Type specimen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_\(biology\)) (AMNH 3982) of *Manospondylus gigas*

A tooth from what is now documented as a *Tyrannosaurus rex* was found in July 1874 upon [South Table Mountain (Colorado)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Table_Mountain_\(Colorado\)) by [Jarvis Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarvis_Hall_\(Colorado\)) student Peter T. Dotson under the auspices of [Arthur Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Lakes) near [Golden, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden,_Colorado), in sediments belonging to the [Denver Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_Formation).[^cite_note-3][^cite_note-\:02-4] In the early 1890s, [John Bell Hatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bell_Hatcher) collected postcranial elements in eastern [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming). The fossils were believed to be from the large species *[Ornithomimus grandis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithomimus)* (now *[Deinodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deinodon)*) but are now considered *T. rex* remains.[^cite_note-quinlanetal2007-5]

In 1892, [Edward Drinker Cope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Drinker_Cope) found two vertebral fragments of a large dinosaur. Cope believed the fragments belonged to an "agathaumid" ([ceratopsid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratopsidae)) dinosaur, and named them *Manospondylus gigas*, meaning "giant porous vertebra", in reference to the numerous openings for blood vessels he found in the bone.[^cite_note-quinlanetal2007-5] The *M. gigas* remains were, in 1907, identified by Hatcher as those of a theropod rather than a ceratopsid.[^cite_note-6]

[Henry Fairfield Osborn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Fairfield_Osborn) recognized the similarity between *Manospondylus gigas* and *T. rex* as early as 1917, by which time the second vertebra had been lost. Owing to the fragmentary nature of the *Manospondylus* vertebrae, Osborn did not synonymize the two genera, instead considering the older genus indeterminate.[^cite_note-osborn1917-7] In June 2000, the [Black Hills Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hills_Institute) found around 10% of a *Tyrannosaurus* skeleton ([BHI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hills_Institute) 6248) at a site that might have been the original *M. gigas* locality.[^cite_note-larson2008-8] While Larson initially considered this specimen as the same animal as the *M. gigas* holotype and suggested that it would take priority over *T. rex*, other researchers including Christopher Brochu remained skeptical that the name *T. rex* would be abandoned.[^cite_note-McTavish2000-9] In 2003, Brochu further argued that although both taxa probably represent the same species, it is impossible to demonstrate that the specimen discovered by the Black Hills Institute represents the same animal as *M. gigas* due to the poor preservation of the *M. gigas* holotype; additionally, following the 2000 [International Code of Zoological Nomenclature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Code_of_Zoological_Nomenclature) amendment, a name in popular usage like *T. rex* has priority over a name with technical priority but not used as a valid name in formal literature after the year 1899 for over fifty years like *M. gigas*.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10][^cite_note-11]

### Skeleton discovery and naming



Outdated skeletal restoration by [William D. Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Diller_Matthew) from 1905, published alongside Osborn's description paper

[Barnum Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barnum_Brown), assistant curator of the [American Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Museum_of_Natural_History), found the first partial skeleton of *T. rex* in eastern Wyoming in 1900. Brown found another partial skeleton in the [Hell Creek Formation](3551034.md) in Montana in 1902, comprising approximately 34 fossilized bones.[^cite_note-osborn1905-12] Writing at the time Brown said "Quarry No. 1 contains the femur, pubes, humerus, three vertebrae and two undetermined bones of a large Carnivorous Dinosaur not described by [Marsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Othniel_Charles_Marsh). ... I have never seen anything like it from the [Cretaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous)."[^cite_note-dingus2010-13] [Henry Fairfield Osborn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Fairfield_Osborn), president of the [American Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Museum_of_Natural_History), named the second skeleton *T. rex* in 1905. The generic name is derived from the [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) words τύραννος (*tyrannos*, meaning "tyrant") and [σαῦρος](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CF%83%CE%B1%E1%BF%A6%CF%81%CE%BF%CF%82) (*sauros*, meaning "lizard"). Osborn used the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language) word *rex*, meaning "king", for the specific name. The full [binomial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binomial_nomenclature) therefore translates to "tyrant lizard the king" or "King Tyrant Lizard", emphasizing the animal's size and presumed dominance over other species of the time.[^cite_note-osborn1905-12]



*Dynamosaurus imperiosus* [holotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holotype) (NHMUK PV R8020, previously AMNH 5886) on display at the [Natural History Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_History_Museum,_London), London

Osborn named the other specimen *Dynamosaurus imperiosus* in a paper in 1905.[^cite_note-osborn1905-12] In 1906, Osborn recognized that the two skeletons were from the same species and selected *Tyrannosaurus* as the preferred name.[^cite_note-osborn1906-14] In 1941, the *T. rex* type specimen was sold to the [Carnegie Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Museum_of_Natural_History) in Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, for \$7,000 (equivalent to \$153,224 in 2025).[^cite_note-dingus2010-13] The original *Dynamosaurus* material now resides in the collections of the [Natural History Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_History_Museum,_London), London.[^cite_note-Breithaupt-15]

From the 1910s through the end of the 1950s, Barnum's discoveries remained the only specimens of *Tyrannosaurus*, as the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) and wars kept many paleontologists out of the field.[^cite_note-larson2008-8]

### Continued study



Specimen "[Sue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_\(dinosaur\))", [Field Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_Museum_of_Natural_History), Chicago (with some bones reconstructed)

Beginning in the 1960s, there was renewed interest in *Tyrannosaurus*, resulting in the recovery of 42 skeletons (5–80% complete by bone count) from western North America.[^cite_note-larson2008-8] In 1967, Dr. William MacMannis located and recovered the skeleton named "MOR 008", which is 15% complete by bone count and has a reconstructed skull displayed at the [Museum of the Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_the_Rockies). The 1990s saw numerous discoveries, with nearly twice as many finds as in all previous years, including two of the most complete skeletons found to date: [Sue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_\(dinosaur\)) and [Stan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stan_\(dinosaur\)).[^cite_note-larson2008-8]

[Sue Hendrickson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Hendrickson) discovered the most complete (approximately 85%) and largest *Tyrannosaurus* skeleton in the [Hell Creek Formation](3551034.md) on August 12, 1990. The specimen Sue, named after the discoverer, was the object of a legal battle over its ownership. In 1997, the litigation was settled in favor of Maurice Williams, the original land owner. The fossil collection was purchased by the [Field Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_Museum_of_Natural_History) at auction for \$7.6 million, making it the most expensive dinosaur skeleton until the sale of Stan for \$31.8 million in 2020.[^cite_note-16] The mounted skeleton opened to the public on May 17, 2000. A study of this specimen's fossilized bones showed that Sue reached full size at age 19 and died at the age of 28, the longest estimated life of any tyrannosaur known.[^cite_note-Ericksonetal2004TyrannosaurGigantism-17]



Replica of "[Scotty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotty_\(dinosaur\))", the largest known specimen, exhibited in Japan

Another *Tyrannosaurus*, nicknamed Stan (NHMAD 2020.00001, formerly BHI 3033[^cite_note-Boeye2026-18]), in honor of amateur paleontologist Stan Sacrison, was recovered from the Hell Creek Formation in 1992. Stan is the second most complete skeleton found, with 199 bones recovered representing 70% of the total.[^cite_note-19] This tyrannosaur also had many bone pathologies, including broken and healed ribs, a broken and healed neck, and a substantial hole in the back of its head, about the size of a *Tyrannosaurus* tooth.[^cite_note-20]

In 1998, 20-year-old Bucky Derflinger noticed a *T. rex* toe exposed above ground, making him the youngest person to discover a *Tyrannosaurus*. The specimen, dubbed [Bucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Specimens_of_Tyrannosaurus#%22Bucky%22:_TCM_2001.90.1) in honor of its discoverer, was a young adult, 3.0 metres (10 ft) tall and 11 metres (35 ft) long. Bucky is the first *Tyrannosaurus* to be found that preserved a [furcula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Furcula) (wishbone). Bucky is permanently displayed at [The Children's Museum of Indianapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Children's_Museum_of_Indianapolis).[^cite_note-21]



The specimens "Sue", AMNH 5027, "Stan", and "Jane", to scale with a human.

In 2000, crews organized by [Jack Horner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Horner_\(paleontologist\)) discovered five *Tyrannosaurus* skeletons near the [Fort Peck Reservoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Peck_Reservoir).[^cite_note-bbc-horner-22] In 2001, a 50% complete skeleton of a juvenile *Tyrannosaurus* was discovered in the Hell Creek Formation by a crew from the [Burpee Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burpee_Museum_of_Natural_History). Dubbed Jane (BMRP 2002.4.1), the find was thought to be the first known skeleton of a [pygmy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pygmy) tyrannosaurid, *[Nanotyrannus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanotyrannus)*, but other researchers considered it as the most complete juvenile *T. rex*;[^cite_note-currieetal2003-23] currently exhibited at the Burpee Museum of Natural History, Jane is now re-described as the holotype of *N. lethaeus*.[^cite_note-Z\&N25-24] In 2002, a skeleton nicknamed "Wyrex", discovered by amateur collectors Dan Wells and Don Wyrick, had 114 bones and was 38% complete. The dig was concluded in 2004 by the [Black Hills Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hills_Institute) with the first live [online](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online) *Tyrannosaurus* excavation.[^cite_note-larson2008-8]

In 2006, [Montana State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_State_University) revealed that it possessed the largest *Tyrannosaurus* skull yet discovered (from a specimen named MOR 008), measuring 5 feet (152 cm) long.[^cite_note-MOR008-25] Subsequent comparisons indicated that the longest head was 136.5 centimetres (53.7 in) (from specimen LACM 23844) and the widest head was 90.2 centimetres (35.5 in) (from Sue).[^cite_note-gignac-26]

## Description

### Size



Size (in blue) compared to select giant theropods and a human

*T. rex* was one of the largest land carnivores of all time. One of its largest and the most complete specimens, [Sue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_\(dinosaur\)) (FMNH PR2081), measured 12.3–12.8 m (40–42 ft) long,[^cite_note-SueFMNH-27][^cite_note-Hutchinsonet.al.2011-28][^cite_note-Holtz2008-29] was 3.66–3.96 m (12.0–13.0 ft) tall at the hips,[^cite_note-SueFMNH-27][^cite_note-Sue-30][^cite_note-FMNH-31] and according to the most recent studies, using a variety of techniques, maximum body masses have been estimated approximately 8.4–9.22 t (8.27–9.07 long tons; 9.26–10.16 short tons).[^cite_note-Persons-32][^cite_note-HartmanMassEstimate-33][^cite_note-nicolas2014-34] A specimen nicknamed [Scotty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotty_\(dinosaur\)) (RSM P2523.8), located at the [Royal Saskatchewan Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saskatchewan_Museum), is reported to measure 13 m (43 ft) in length. Using a mass estimation technique that extrapolates from the [circumference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumference) of the femur, Scotty was estimated as the largest known specimen at 8.87 t (8.73 long tons; 9.78 short tons) in body mass.[^cite_note-Persons-32][^cite_note-Lyle2019-35]

Not every adult *Tyrannosaurus* specimen recovered is as big. Historically average adult mass estimates have varied widely over the years, from as low as 4.5 t (4.4 long tons; 5.0 short tons),[^cite_note-andersonetal1985-36][^cite_note-bakker1986-37] to more than 7.2 t (7.1 long tons; 7.9 short tons),[^cite_note-henderson1999-38] with most modern estimates ranging between 5.4 and 8.0 t (5.3 and 7.9 long tons; 6.0 and 8.8 short tons).[^cite_note-Hutchinsonet.al.2011-28][^cite_note-ericksonetal2004-39][^cite_note-farlowetal1995-40][^cite_note-seebacher2001-41][^cite_note-christiansenfarina2004-42]

A 2024 study found that there was little evidence of size-based sexual dimorphism in *T. rex*.[^cite_note-Mallon\&Hone24-43]

### Skull



Skull of specimen AMNH 5027 with labelled diagrams in dorsal, lateral, anterior, posterior, and medial (lower jaw) views

The largest known *T. rex* skulls measure up to 1.52 m (5 ft 0 in) in length.[^cite_note-MOR008-25][^cite_note-SueFMNH-27] Large [fenestrae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenestra_\(anatomy\)) (openings) in the skull reduced weight, as in all carnivorous theropods. In other respects *Tyrannosaurus*'s skull was significantly different from those of large non-[tyrannosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosaurid) theropods. It was extremely wide at the rear but had a narrow snout, allowing unusually good [binocular vision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binocular_vision).[^cite_note-Stevens2006Binocular-44][^cite_note-jaffe-45] The skull bones were massive and the [nasals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasal_bone) and some other bones were fused, preventing movement between them; but many were [pneumatized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pneumatized_bones) (contained a "honeycomb" of tiny air spaces) and thus lighter. These and other skull-strengthening features are part of the [tyrannosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosaurid) trend towards an increasingly powerful bite, which easily surpassed that of all non-tyrannosaurids.[^cite_note-SnivelyHendersonPhillips2006FusedVaultedNasals-46][^cite_note-MM03-47][^cite_note-GEetal96-48] The tip of the upper jaw was U-shaped (most non-tyrannosauroid carnivores had V-shaped upper jaws), which increased the amount of tissue and bone a tyrannosaur could rip out with one bite, although it also increased the stresses on the front teeth.[^cite_note-holtz1994-49]

The teeth of *T. rex* displayed marked [heterodonty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterodont) (differences in shape).[^cite_note-brochu2003-10][^cite_note-Smith2005HeterodontyTRex-50] The [premaxillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premaxilla) teeth, four per side at the front of the upper jaw, were closely packed, *D*-shaped in cross-section, had reinforcing ridges on the rear surface, were [incisiform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incisor) (their tips were chisel-like blades) and curved backwards. The *D*-shaped cross-section, reinforcing ridges and backwards curve reduced the risk that the teeth would snap when *Tyrannosaurus* bit and pulled. The remaining teeth were robust, like "lethal bananas" rather than daggers, more widely spaced and also had reinforcing ridges.[^cite_note-New_Scientist1998DinosaurDetectives-51] Those in the upper jaw, twelve per side in mature individuals,[^cite_note-brochu2003-10] were larger than their counterparts of the lower jaw, except at the rear. The largest found so far is estimated to have been 30.5 cm (12.0 in) long including the root when the animal was alive, making it the largest tooth of any carnivorous dinosaur yet found.[^cite_note-SueFMNH2-52] The lower jaw was robust. Its front [dentary bone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dentary_bone) bore thirteen teeth. Behind the tooth row, the lower jaw became notably taller.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10] The upper and lower jaws of *Tyrannosaurus*, like those of many dinosaurs, possessed numerous [foramina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foramen), or small holes in the bone. Various functions have been proposed for these foramina, such as a crocodile-like sensory system[^cite_note-carr2017-53] or evidence of [extra-oral structures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integument) such as scales or potentially lips,[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-\:0-55][^cite_note-MontevialeCroc-56] with subsequent research on theropod tooth wear patterns supporting such a proposition.[^cite_note-CullenEtAl23-57]

### Skeleton



[Life restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleoart) showing scaly skin with sparse feathering, and lipped jaws



Skeletal reconstruction of specimen "Sue"

The [vertebral column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebral_column) of *Tyrannosaurus* consisted of ten neck vertebrae, thirteen back vertebrae and five sacral vertebrae. The number of tail vertebrae is unknown and could well have varied between individuals but probably numbered at least forty. Sue was mounted with forty-seven of such caudal vertebrae.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10] The neck of *T. rex* formed a natural S-shaped curve like that of other theropods. Compared to these, it was exceptionally short, deep and muscular to support the massive head. The second vertebra, the axis, was especially short. The remaining neck vertebrae were weakly opisthocoelous, i.e. with a convex front of the vertebral body and a concave rear. The vertebral bodies had single pleurocoels, pneumatic depressions created by [air sacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_sac), on their sides.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10] The vertebral bodies of the torso were robust but with a narrow waist. Their undersides were keeled. The front sides were concave with a deep vertical trough. They had large pleurocoels. Their neural spines had very rough front and rear sides for the attachment of strong tendons. The sacral vertebrae were fused to each other, both in their vertebral bodies and neural spines. They were pneumatized. They were connected to the pelvis by transverse processes and sacral ribs. The tail was heavy and moderately long, in order to balance the massive head and torso and to provide space for massive [locomotor muscles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_locomotion) that attached to the thighbones. The thirteenth tail vertebra formed the transition point between the deep tail base and the middle tail that was stiffened by a long front articulation processes. The underside of the trunk was covered by eighteen or nineteen pairs of segmented belly ribs.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10]



Right forelimb of specimen "Sue"

The [shoulder girdle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoulder_girdle) was longer than the entire forelimb. The shoulder blade had a narrow shaft but was exceptionally expanded at its upper end. It connected via a long forward protrusion to the [coracoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coracoid), which was rounded. Both shoulder blades were connected by a small [furcula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Furcula). The paired breast bones possibly were made of [cartilage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartilage) only.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10]

The forelimb or arm was very short. The upper arm bone, the humerus, was short but robust. It had a narrow upper end with an exceptionally rounded head. The lower arm bones, the [ulna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulna) and radius, were straight elements, much shorter than the humerus. The second [metacarpal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metacarpus) was longer and wider than the first, whereas normally in theropods the opposite is true. The forelimbs had only two clawed fingers,[^cite_note-brochu2003-10] along with an additional splint-like small third [metacarpal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metacarpus) representing the remnant of a third digit.[^cite_note-CLKC08-58]



Pelvic girdle of specimen MOR 555

The [pelvis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelvis) was a large structure. Its upper bone, the [ilium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilium_\(bone\)), was both very long and high, providing an extensive attachment area for hindlimb muscles. The front [pubic bone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pubic_bone) ended in an enormous pubic boot, longer than the entire shaft of the element. The rear [ischium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ischium) was slender and straight, pointing obliquely to behind and below.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10]

In contrast to the arms, the hindlimbs were among the longest in proportion to body size of any theropod. In the foot, the [metatarsus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metatarsus) was "arctometatarsalian", meaning that the part of the third metatarsal near the ankle was pinched. The third metatarsal was also exceptionally sinuous.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10] Compensating for the immense bulk of the animal, many bones throughout the skeleton were hollowed, reducing its weight without significant loss of strength.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10]

## Classification



Skull casts of different *Tyrannosaurus* specimens

*Tyrannosaurus* is the [type](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_\(biology\)) genus of the superfamily [Tyrannosauroidea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosauroidea), the family [Tyrannosauridae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosauridae), and the subfamily Tyrannosaurinae; in other words it is the standard by which paleontologists decide whether to include other species in the group. Other members of the tyrannosaurine subfamily include the North American *[Daspletosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daspletosaurus)* and the Asian *[Tarbosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarbosaurus)*,[^cite_note-currieetal2003-23][^cite_note-holtz2004-59] both of which have occasionally been synonymized with *Tyrannosaurus*.[^cite_note-paul1988-60]

Tyrannosaurids were once commonly thought to be descendants of earlier large theropods such as [megalosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinosauroidea) and [carnosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnosauria), although more recently they were reclassified with the generally smaller [coelurosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coelurosauria).[^cite_note-holtz1994-49] The earliest tyrannosaur group were the crested proceratosaurids, while later and more derived members belong to the [Pantyrannosauria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantyrannosauria). Tyrannosaurs started out as small theropods; at least some became larger by the [Early Cretaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Cretaceous). It is likely that tyrannosauroids rose to prominence after the decline in allosauroid and megalosauroid diversity seen during the early stages of the Late Cretaceous.[^cite_note-61]

Tyrannosauroids are characterized by their fused nasals and dental arrangement. Pantyrannosaurs are characterized by unique features in their hips as well as an enlarged foramen in the quadrate, a broad postorbital and hourglass shaped nasals. Some of the more derived pantyrannosaurs lack nasal pneumaticity and have a lower humerus to femur ratio with their arms starting to see some reduction. Some pantyrannosaurs started developing an arctometatarsus. Eutyrannosaurs have a rough texture on their nasal bones and their mandibular fenestra is reduced externally. Tyrannosaurids lack kinetic skulls or special crests on their nasal bones, and have a lacrimal with a distinctive process on it. Tyrannosaurids also have an interfenestral strut that is less than half as big as the maxillary fenestra.[^cite_note-62]

Many [phylogenetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogeny) analyses have found *[Tarbosaurus bataar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarbosaurus_bataar)* to be the [sister taxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_taxon) of *T. rex*.[^cite_note-holtz2004-59] The discovery of the tyrannosaurid *[Lythronax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lythronax)* further indicates that *Tarbosaurus* and *Tyrannosaurus* are closely related, forming a clade with fellow Asian tyrannosaurid *[Zhuchengtyrannus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuchengtyrannus)*, with *Lythronax* being their sister taxon.[^cite_note-Loewen13-63][^cite_note-64] A further study from 2016 by Steve Brusatte, Thomas Carr and colleagues, also indicates that *Tyrannosaurus* may have been an immigrant from Asia, as well as a possible descendant of *Tarbosaurus*.[^cite_note-65]

This cladogram of Tyrannosauridae is based on the [phylogenetic analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogenetic_analysis) conducted by Loewen and colleagues in 2013.[^cite_note-Loewen13-63]

| [Tyrannosauridae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosauridae) | [Albertosaurinae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albertosaurinae)       *[Gorgosaurus libratus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorgosaurus_libratus)*       *[Albertosaurus sarcophagus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albertosaurus_sarcophagus)*        [Tyrannosaurinae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosaurinae)       [Dinosaur Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_Park_Formation) tyrannosaurid           *[Daspletosaurus torosus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daspletosaurus_torosus)*            [Two Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Medicine_Formation) tyrannosaurid           *[Teratophoneus curriei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teratophoneus_curriei)*            *[Bistahieversor sealeyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bistahieversor_sealeyi)*            *[Lythronax argestes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lythronax_argestes)*            ***Tyrannosaurus rex***            *[Tarbosaurus bataar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarbosaurus_bataar)*       *[Zhuchengtyrannus magnus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuchengtyrannus_magnus)* |
| --- | --- |
|  |  |

In their 2024 description of *Tyrannosaurus mcraeensis*, Dalman et al. recovered similar results to previous analyses, with *Tyrannosaurus* as the sister taxon to the clade formed by *Tarbosaurus* and *Zhuchengtyrannus*, called the Tyrannosaurini. They also found support for a [monophyletic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monophyletic) clade containing *Daspletosaurus* and *[Thanatotheristes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanatotheristes)*, typically referred to as the [Daspletosaurini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daspletosaurini).[^cite_note-T.mcraeensis-66][^cite_note-SV24-67]

|  | *[Albertosaurus sarcophagus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albertosaurus_sarcophagus)*       *[Gorgosaurus libratus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorgosaurus_libratus)*                         *[Daspletosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daspletosaurus) horneri*      *[Thanatotheristes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanatotheristes)*               *Daspletosaurus torosus*       *Daspletosaurus wilsoni*                 *[Teratophoneus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teratophoneus)*            *[Nanuqsaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanuqsaurus)*            *[Bistahieversor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bistahieversor)*            *[Lythronax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lythronax)*     [Tyrannosaurini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosaurini)            ***Tyrannosaurus mcraeensis***       ***Tyrannosaurus rex***               *[Zhuchengtyrannus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuchengtyrannus)*       *[Tarbosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarbosaurus)* |
| --- | --- |
|  |  |

### Additional species



Diagram showing the differences between a generalized *Tarbosaurus* (A) and *Tyrannosaurus rex* (B) skull

In 1955, Soviet [paleontologist](23084.md) [Evgeny Maleev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evgeny_Maleev) named a new species, *Tyrannosaurus bataar*, from [Mongolia](19271.md).[^cite_note-maleev1955-68] By 1965, this species was renamed as a distinct genus, *Tarbosaurus bataar*.[^cite_note-rozhdestvensky1965-69] While most paleontologists continue to maintain the two as distinct genera, some authors such as [Thomas Holtz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Holtz), [Kenneth Carpenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_Carpenter), and [Thomas Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Carr_\(paleontologist\)) argue that the two species are similar enough to be considered members of the same genus, restoring the Mongolian [taxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxon)'s original [binomial name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binomial_nomenclature).[^cite_note-holtz1994-49][^cite_note-carpenter1992-70][^cite_note-carr2017-53]

Some specimens from the Late Cretaceous deposits of [China](5405.md) have been described as new species of *Tyrannosaurus*: *T.* "lanpingensis" based on isolated [lateral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lateral_\(anatomy\)) tooth from the red beds of [Yunnan](166410.md) in 1975; *T.* "turpanensis" from the [Subashi Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subashi_Formation), [Turpan Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan_Basin), [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) in 1978; and *T. luanchuanensis* from the [Quiba Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiba_Formation) in [Henan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henan_Province) in 1979–1980.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-Feng2016-72][^cite_note-Lucas2018-73] The former two taxa were published without detailed descriptions and are therefore nomina nuda. *T.* "turpanensis" and *T. luanchuanensis* were tentatively listed as [junior synonyms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synonym_\(taxonomy\)) of *Tarbosaurus bataar* by Holtz in 2004; Holtz also listed *T.* "lanpingensis," but as a nomen dubium.[^cite_note-holtz2004-59][^cite_note-Feng2016-72][^cite_note-Lucas2018-73]

VGI, no. 231/3, a large [phalanx bone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phalanx_bone), assigned to *Tyrannosaurus* sp. by Yarkov in 2000, was found in the Lower Maastrichtian of [Bereslavka, Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bereslavka,_Russia). In 2004, Averianov and Yarkov reinterpreted it as a [metacarpal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metacarpal) or [metatarsal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metatarsal) that possibly belongs to [ceratosaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratosaur).[^cite_note-74] In their 2023 overview, Averianov and Lopatin mention this specimen as well as a single tooth from the same site only as Theropoda indet.[^cite_note-75]

In 2001, various tyrannosaurid teeth and a metatarsal unearthed in a quarry near [Zhucheng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhucheng), China, were assigned by Chinese paleontologist [Hu Chengzhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Chengzhi) to the newly erected species *Tyrannosaurus zhuchengensis*. However, in a nearby site, a right maxilla and left jawbone were assigned to the newly erected tyrannosaurid genus *Zhuchengtyrannus* in 2011. It is possible that *T. zhuchengensis* is [synonymous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synonym_\(taxonomy\)) with *Zhuchengtyrannus*. In any case, *T. zhuchengensis* is considered to be a *[nomen dubium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomen_dubium)* as the holotype lacks [diagnostic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diagnosis_\(taxonomy\)) features below the level Tyrannosaurinae.[^cite_note-76]

In 2006, a fragmentary tyrannosaurid lacrimal (CM 9401) from the [Judith River Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_River_Formation) of Fergus County, Montana, was described as ?*Tyrannosaurus* sp. This isolated right lacrimal was originally collected alongside the holotype specimen of *Deinosuchus rugosus*, a giant crocodilian, and remained undescribed until its re-identification as belonging to a tyrannosaurid theropod in the 1980s by paleontologist [Dale Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Russell). The lacrimal closely resembles those of *Tyrannosaurus rex* in both size and morphology. Notably, it lacks the "lacrimal horn" typically present in earlier tyrannosaurids like *[Albertosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albertosaurus)* and *[Gorgosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorgosaurus)*, instead exhibiting a distinct rugosity along the dorsal surface—consistent with *T. rex* and its Asian relative *Tarbosaurus*. The specimen's considerable size places it within the range of known *T. rex* individuals, suggesting the presence of large tyrannosaurids during the Campanian stage (\~75 million years ago), a temporal range earlier than the established Maastrichtian age (\~68–66 Ma) for *Tyrannosaurus rex*. However, the exact age and provenance of CM 9401 remain uncertain due to a lack of detailed field documentation.[^cite_note-77]

In 2018, a paper describing tyrannosaurid teeth from the Two Medicine Formation noted a premaxillary tooth (YPM VPPU 023469) had a strong resemblance to the teeth of [Sue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_\(Tyrannosaurus\)) to the exclusion of any Campanian tyrannosaurid. Additionally, the authors of this paper suggested that CM 9401 also comes from the Two Medicine Formation, as there were preservational similarities between its locality and the Willow Creek anticline, which is where the tooth was found.[^cite_note-78] Notably, this would place both specimens in the Flag Butte Member of the Two Medicine Formation, which dates from 77 to 76.3 Ma, far older than any other *Tyrannosaurus* specimen, and directly contemporaneous with *Daspletosaurus.* In 2025, these specimens, with their old geologic age, were used as evidence by Charlie Scherer to suggest that the Tyrannosaurini did not evolve directly from *Daspletosaurus*.[^cite_note-Scherer2025-79]

### *Tyrannosaurus imperator* and *Tyrannosaurus regina*

In a 2022 study, [Gregory S. Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_S._Paul) and colleagues argued that *Tyrannosaurus rex*, as traditionally understood, actually represents three species: the type species *Tyrannosaurus rex*, and two previously unrecognized species: *T. imperator* (meaning "tyrant lizard emperor") and *T. regina* (meaning "tyrant lizard queen"). The holotype of the former (*T. imperator*) is the [Sue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_\(dinosaur\)) specimen, and the holotype of the latter (*T. regina*) is [Wankel rex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wankel_rex). The division into multiple species was primarily based on the high degree of variation in the proportions and robusticity of the [femur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femur) (and other skeletal elements) across *T. rex* specimens. Presumed robust and gracile morphotypes and the number of small incisiform teeth in the dentary were also used as a line of evidence. Paul and colleagues categorized the species as follows: *T. rex* demonstrates robust anatomy, a moderate ratio of femur length vs circumference, and the possession of a singular slender incisiform dentary tooth, *T. imperator* is robust with a small femur length to circumference ratio and two of the slender teeth, and *T. regina* is more gracile with a high femur ratio and one of the slender teeth.[^cite_note-paul2022-80]

However, several leading paleontologists, including [Stephen Brusatte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Brusatte), [Thomas Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Carr_\(paleontologist\)), [Thomas Holtz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Holtz), David Hone, [Jingmai O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jingmai_O'Connor), and [Lindsay Zanno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindsay_Zanno), criticized the study or expressed skepticism of its conclusions.[^cite_note-newyorktimes-81][^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-Carr_et.al.(2022)-84] Holtz and Zanno both remarked that it was plausible that more than one species of *Tyrannosaurus* existed, but felt the new study was insufficient to support the species it proposed. Holtz remarked that, even if *Tyrannosaurus imperator* represented a distinct species from *Tyrannosaurus rex*, it may represent the same species as *Nanotyrannus lancensis* and would need to be called *Tyrannosaurus lancensis*. O'Connor, a curator at the Field Museum, where the *T. imperator* holotype Sue is displayed, regarded the new species as too poorly-supported to justify modifying the exhibit signs. Brusatte, Carr, and O'Connor viewed the distinguishing features proposed between the species as reflecting natural variation within a species. Both Carr and O'Connor expressed concerns about the study's inability to determine which of the proposed species several well-preserved specimens belonged to. Another paleontologist, [Philip J. Currie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_J._Currie), originally co-authored the study but withdrew from it as he did not want to be involved in naming the new species.[^cite_note-newyorktimes-81]

In a subsequent paper published in 2025, Paul maintained the validity of *T. imperator* and *T. regina*, arguing that these proposed species could be further distinguished by the shape of their postorbital bosses (bony rugosities behind the eyes).[^cite_note-Paul2025-85]

### *Tyrannosaurus mcraeensis*





Reconstructed skull (top) and speculative [life restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_restoration) (bottom) of *T. mcraeensis*

In 2024, Dalman and colleagues [described](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Species_description) the remains of a tyrannosaur discovered in 1983 in the Campanian-early Maastrichtian [Hall Lake Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hall_Lake_Formation) in New Mexico. Reposited at the [New Mexico Museum of Natural History and Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Museum_of_Natural_History_and_Science), the fossil material (NMMNH P-3698) consists of the right [postorbital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postorbital), right [squamosal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squamosal), left [palatine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palatine), and an incomplete [maxilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxilla) from the skull, the left [dentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dentary), right [splenial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Splenial), right prearticular, right [angular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_bone) and right [articular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articular) from the lower jaws, isolated teeth, and [chevrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haemal_arch).[^cite_note-T.mcraeensis-66] Some of the bones were briefly mentioned in 1984 as belonging to *T. rex*,[^cite_note-Lozinsky1984-86] and described in 1986.[^cite_note-Gillette1986-87]

Lehman and Carpenter (1990) suggested that NMMNH P-3698 belonged to a new tyrannosaurid genus,[^cite_note-88] while Carr and Williamson (2000) disagreed with their claim.[^cite_note-89] Sullivan and Lucas (2015) argued that there is little evidence to support NMMNH P-3698 as a specimen of *Tyrannosaurus rex*, so they tentatively classified it as cf. *Tyrannosaurus* sp.; they also considered that the McRae tyrannosaur lived before the [Lancian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancian) (before 67 million years ago) based on its probable association with the *[Alamosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alamosaurus)* fossil.[^cite_note-90]



Mandibular elements of the *T. mcraeensis* holotype

Dalman et al. (2024) proposed the new name *Tyrannosaurus mcraeensis* for the holotype (NMMNH P-3698), referencing the McRae Group, the rock layers to which the Hall Lake Formation belongs. The holotype of *T. mcraeensis* is found in the strata that are around a few million years older than the accepted range of *T. rex*, which existed at the end of the Maastrichtian. The rock layers were initially estimated to date to between 72.7 and 70.9 Ma, correlating to the latest Campanian or earliest Maastrichtian.[^cite_note-T.mcraeensis-66] However, in a 2024 conference [abstract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_\(summary\)), the specific stratigraphic layer which yielded *T. mcraeensis* was estimated to be younger than 69.0 ± 0.4 Ma and older than 66.0 Ma based on the sandstone from the same fossil locality,[^cite_note-91] which would suggest that the age of *T. mcraeensis* is much closer to *T. rex* than previously thought.[^cite_note-Scherer2025-79] *T. mcraeensis* was estimated at 12 metres (39 ft) long, which is similar to the size of an adult *T. rex*. The two are distinguished by characters of the skull. Amongst these, the dentary of *T. mcraeensis* is proportionately longer and possesses a less prominent chin, and the lower jaw shallower than that of *T. rex*, suggesting a weaker bite. The teeth are likewise blunter and more laterally compressed, while the post orbital crests are less prominent. Likewise, the skeletal anatomy showcases shared characteristics with *Tarbosaurus* and *Zhuchengtyrannus*.[^cite_note-T.mcraeensis-66][^cite_note-NYT-20240111-92]

The validity of *T. mcraeensis* was questioned by other researchers in 2025. Morrison and colleagues noted the uncertainty in the previous age estimate and argued that the inclusion of the titanosaurian fossil (cf. *Alamosaurus*) as a biostratigraphic marker of the Hall Lake Formation fauna makes the dating problematic due to the fossil having a huge variability in assumed sedimentation rates. The study also suggested the uncertainty in the morphology of *T. mcraeensis* holotype being reliably outside the known variation of *T. rex*. Regardless of the age and validity of *T. mcraeensis*, the authors concluded that the genus *Tyrannosaurus* still most likely originated from Laramidia and that the ancestors of this genus possibly migrated from Asia.[^cite_note-93] Later that year, Voris and colleagues regarded *T. mcraeensis* as a [junior synonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junior_synonym) of *T. rex*, agreeing with the problematic stratigraphic data and finding all of the proposed diagnostic characters of *T. mcraeensis* to be in the known *T. rex* specimen range of variation.[^cite_note-voris25-94] In his study of the *T. rex* holotype skull, Carr also did not regard *T. mcraeensis* as a valid species, agreeing with the proposed characters of the *T. mcraeensis* holotype being visible throughout the hypodigm of *T. rex*.[^cite_note-95]

### *Nanotyrannus*

Main article: [Nanotyrannus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanotyrannus)



Cast of CMNH 7541, the [holotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holotype) of *Nanotyrannus lancensis*, sometimes interpreted as a juvenile *Tyrannosaurus*.

Other tyrannosaur fossils found in the same formations as *T. rex* were originally classified as separate taxa, including *Aublysodon* and *Albertosaurus megagracilis*,[^cite_note-paul1988-60] the latter being named *Dinotyrannus megagracilis* in 1995.[^cite_note-Olshevsky1995-96] These fossils are now universally considered to belong to juvenile *T. rex*.[^cite_note-carrwilliamson2004-97] A small but nearly complete skull from Montana, 57.2 centimeters (1.88 ft) long, is an exception. This skull, [CMNH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Museum_of_Natural_History) 7541, was originally classified as a species of *[Gorgosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorgosaurus)* (*G. lancensis*) by [Charles W. Gilmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_W._Gilmore) in 1946.[^cite_note-gilmore1946-98] In 1988, the specimen was re-described by [Robert T. Bakker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_T._Bakker), Michael Williams, then the curator of paleontology at the Cleveland Museum of Natural History, and [Phil Currie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_J._Currie), where the original specimen was housed and is now on display. Their initial research indicated that the skull bones were fused, and that it therefore represented an adult specimen. In light of this, Bakker and colleagues assigned the skull to a new genus named *Nanotyrannus* (meaning "dwarf tyrant", for its apparently small adult size).[^cite_note-bakkeretal1988-99] However, in 1999, a detailed analysis by [Thomas Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Carr_\(paleontologist\)) revealed the specimen to be a juvenile, leading Carr and many other paleontologists to consider it a juvenile *T. rex* individual.[^cite_note-carr1999-100][^cite_note-juvenilebataar-101]



Reconstructed skeleton of "Jane", [Burpee Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burpee_Museum_of_Natural_History)

In 2001, a more complete juvenile tyrannosaur (nicknamed "Jane", [accession number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accession_number_\(cultural_property\)) BMRP 2002.4.1), suggested to belong to the same species as the original *Nanotyrannus* specimen, was uncovered. This discovery prompted a conference on tyrannosaurs focused on the issues of the validity of *Nanotyrannus*, held at the [Burpee Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burpee_Museum_of_Natural_History) in 2005. Several paleontologists who had previously published opinions that *N. lancensis* was a valid species, including Currie and Williams, saw the discovery of "Jane" as a confirmation that *Nanotyrannus* was, in fact, a juvenile *T. rex*.[^cite_note-currie2003a-102][^cite_note-currieetal2005-103][^cite_note-henderson2005-104] [Peter Larson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Larson) continued to support the hypothesis that *Nanotyrannus lancensis* was a separate but closely related species, and also argued that *[Stygivenator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stygivenator)* (LACM 28471), which is generally considered to be a juvenile of *Tyrannosaurus rex*, could be a younger specimen of *Nanotyrannus*.[^cite_note-larson13-105][^cite_note-106]

In 2011, news reports about a 2006 discovery of a new, virtually complete theropod specimen found alongside a [ceratopsid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratopsid) were made. The specimens were studied by Robert Bakker and Peter Larson on-site, who identified the ceratopsian as *[Triceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops)* and the theropod as *Nanotyrannus*.[^cite_note-Nano_Smithsonian_2011-107] It was impossible to determine whether the theropod specimen, nicknamed "Bloody Mary", was distinct from *T. rex*, as the specimen remained in private hands until 2020, when the ownership of the specimen was decided by the [Montana Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Supreme_Court) to be given to the landowners Mary Anne and Lige Murray.[^cite_note-108] The fossil was acquired by the [North Carolina Museum of Natural Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Museum_of_Natural_Sciences) in 2020.[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110] In 2025, paleontologists [Lindsay Zanno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindsay_Zanno) and James Napoli published an initial description of the "Bloody Mary" specimen as part of an extensive revision of the genus *Nanotyrannus*. They identified this specimen as a skeletally mature individual of *Nanotyrannus* and provided several lines of evidence supporting the validity of this genus. They also described a second species, *N. lethaeus*, based on the Jane specimen due to notable differences between it and *N. lancensis*.[^cite_note-Z\&N25-24]

## Paleobiology

### Life history



Skull of a juvenile *T. rex* formerly classified as *Dinotyrannus megagracilis*

The identification of several specimens as juvenile *T. rex* has allowed scientists to document [ontogenetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ontogeny) changes in the species, estimate the lifespan, and determine how quickly the animals would have grown. The *Stygivenator* holotype ([LACM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_County_Museum_of_Natural_History) 28471, the "Jordan theropod"), possibly a juvenile *T. rex*, is estimated to have weighed only 30 kg (66 lb), while the largest adults, such as [FMNH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_Museum_of_Natural_History) PR2081 (Sue) most likely weighed about 5,650 kg (12,460 lb). [Histologic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histology) analysis of *T. rex* bones showed LACM 28471 had aged only 2 years when it died. Sue was initially estimated at 28 years old, an age which was at the time considered close to the maximum for the species,[^cite_note-ericksonetal2004-39] but the most recent studies estimate Sue's age at 27–33 years.[^cite_note-111] Similarly, [Trix (RGM 792.000)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trix_\(dinosaur\)) is estimated to have been at least 30 years old at time of death.[^cite_note-112]

Histology has also allowed the age of other specimens to be determined. Growth curves can be developed when the ages of different specimens are plotted on a graph along with their mass. A *T. rex* growth curve is S-shaped, with juveniles remaining under 1,800 kg (4,000 lb) until approximately 14 years of age, when body size began to increase dramatically. During this rapid growth phase, a young *T. rex* would gain an average of 600 kg (1,300 lb) a year for the next four years. At 18 years of age, the curve plateaus again, indicating that growth slowed dramatically. For example, only 600 kg (1,300 lb) separated the 28-year-old Sue from a 22-year-old Canadian specimen ([RTMP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Tyrrell_Museum_of_Palaeontology) 81.12.1).[^cite_note-ericksonetal2004-39] A 2004 histological study performed by different workers corroborates these results, finding that rapid growth began to slow at around 16 years of age.[^cite_note-hornerpadian2004-113]



A graph showing the hypothesized growth curve, body mass versus age (drawn in black, with other tyrannosaurids for comparison), based on Erickson and colleagues 2004. Studies like this are now known to have been skewed by the inclusion of specimens now considered *Nanotyrannus*

A study by Hutchinson and colleagues in 2011 corroborated the previous estimation methods in general, but their estimation of peak growth rates is significantly higher; it found that the "maximum growth rates for T. rex during the exponential stage are 1790 kg/year".[^cite_note-Hutchinsonet.al.2011-28] Although these results were much higher than previous estimations, the authors noted that these results significantly lowered the great difference between its actual growth rate and the one which would be expected of an animal of its size.[^cite_note-Hutchinsonet.al.2011-28] The sudden change in growth rate at the end of the growth spurt may indicate physical maturity, a hypothesis which is supported by the discovery of medullary tissue in the [femur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femur) of a 16 to 20-year-old *T. rex* from Montana ([MOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_the_Rockies) 1125, also known as [B-rex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B-rex)). Medullary tissue is found only in female birds during ovulation, indicating that B-rex was of reproductive age.[^cite_note-schweitzeretal2005-114] Further study indicates an age of 18 for this specimen.[^cite_note-LW08-115] In 2016, it was finally confirmed by Mary Higby Schweitzer and Lindsay Zanno and colleagues that the soft tissue within the femur of MOR 1125 was medullary tissue. This also confirmed the identity of the specimen as a female. The discovery of [medullary bone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medullary_bone) tissue within *Tyrannosaurus* may prove valuable in determining the sex of other dinosaur species in future examinations, as the chemical makeup of medullary tissue is unmistakable.[^cite_note-116] Other tyrannosaurids exhibit extremely similar growth curves, although with lower growth rates corresponding to their lower adult sizes.[^cite_note-ericksonetal2006-117]

An additional study published in 2020 by Woodward and colleagues, for the journal *Science Advances* indicates that during their growth from juvenile to adult, *Tyrannosaurus* was capable of slowing down its growth to counter environmental factors such as lack of food. Based on BMRP 2002.4.1 and BMRP 2006.4.4 between 13 and 15 years old housed at the Burpee Museum in Illinois then referred to as juvenile *Tyrannosaurus* specimens, the authors suggested that the rate of maturation for *Tyrannosaurus* was dependent on resource abundance. This study also indicates that in such changing environments, *Tyrannosaurus* was particularly well-suited to an environment that shifted yearly in regards to resource abundance, hinting that other midsize predators might have had difficulty surviving in such harsh conditions and explaining the niche partitioning between juvenile and adult tyrannosaurs. The study further suggested that *Tyrannosaurus* and *Nanotyrannus* are synonymous, due to analysis of the growth rings in the bones of the two specimens studied.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119]

In January 2026, Woodward, Myhrvold and Horner performed a comprehensive histological analysis of 17 tyrannosaur specimens, and argued that *Tyrannosaurus* likely experienced a more gradual annual growth rate slope than indicated by earlier studies and evidence of a protracted subadult stage, reaching asymptotic size at around 35–40 years of age. The upper limit of the annual growth rate estimate is approximately 43–53 years. They also found that the growth trajectories of BMRP 2002.4.1 and BMRP 2006.4.4 do not fit with other *Tyrannosaurus* specimens in their growth curve model. While they acknowledged the possibility of these ontogenetically immature specimens representing *Nanotyrannus* as suggested by Zanno and Napoli (2025), they noted that the inconsistencies of these specimens observed in the growth curve do not necessarily bear weight on the proposal that *Nanotyrannus* is a distinct taxon.[^cite_note-120]

In July 2026, Longrich and colleagues described a third metatarsal bone of perinate (hatchling) *Tyrannosaurus rex* and cf. *Gorgosaurus libratus*, RSKM P2416.82 from the [Frenchman Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frenchman_Formation) and TMP 1981.16.475 from the [Dinosaur Park Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_Park_Formation) respectively. Histological analyses reveal that lines of arrested growth are absent in both specimens, indicative of hatchling or embryonic stage. Synchrotron scans of each specimen reveal the relatively high density of the outer surface and the pattern changes in cortical (outer layer) bone structure, with a layer similar to the hatchling/[neonatal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neonate) line of modern oviparous (egg-laying) and viviparous (giving live birth) taxa that characterize the transition from embryonic to hatchling stage. The scans also detected evidence of Haversian bone remodeling, the continuous process of bone tissue breaking down and rebuilding to form secondary canals (tubular channels connecting bones), which are indicative of hatchling stage and [precociality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precociality), relative maturity and mobility at birth. The layer lining the [medullary cavity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medullary_cavity) of both specimens likely represent endosteal bone deposition, during which new bone matrix is deposited on the inner surface of the cortical bone. The authors also described a partial dentary (FMNH PR 4920) from the Hell Creek Formation and isolated teeth from the Lance Formation as hatchling *T. rex* and interpret their tooth wear as evidence of consuming relatively large vertebrates.[^cite_note-Longrich2026-121]

The body length and body mass of the hatchling *T. rex* (RSKM P2416.82) are estimated to be approximately 75 cm (2.46 ft) and 2.5 kg (5.5 lb) respectively, and those of the hatchling cf. *G. libratus* (TMP 1981.16.475) are estimated to be approximately 70 cm (2.3 ft) and 2.4 kg (5.3 lb) respectively. On the basis of these body size estimates, Longrich et al. (2026) suggested that the clutch of the confirmed, reproductively mature female *T. rex* (MOR 1125) would have weighed at least 54–60 kg (119–132 lb) which would possibly correspond to 21-32 eggs. They further stated that larger clutch sizes are also plausible, indicating that tyrannosaurids possibly employed [R-selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-selection), a reproductive strategy that maximizes the number of offsprings through large clutch size and minimal parental investment, which would have resulted in rapid maturation of the hatchlings, slower than that of modern birds. It is also likely that the reproduction strategy of tyrannosaurids was an intermediate between R-selection and [K-selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K-selection), the latter of which would maximize parental investment with fewer number of offsprings and delayed maturation. Given the uniparental care observed in modern crocodilians and basal (early-diverging) birds, most non-avian dinosaurs including *T. rex* were likely uniparental as well. The authors also estimated that the relative size of tyrannosaurid hatchlings and eggs are larger than that of squamates, turtles, crocodilians, pterosaurs, and certain dinosaur clades (protoceratopsids and basal sauropodomorphs), but smaller than that of eumaniraptorans and modern birds, probably weighing less in both relative and absolute size than those of the [elephant bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elephant_bird) *[Aepyornis maximus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aepyornis_maximus)* and the [South Island giant moa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island_giant_moa).[^cite_note-Longrich2026-121]

Over half of the known *T. rex* specimens appear to have died within six years of reaching sexual maturity, a pattern which is also seen in other tyrannosaurs and in some large, long-lived birds and mammals today. These species are characterized by high infant mortality rates, followed by relatively low mortality among juveniles. Mortality increases again following sexual maturity, partly due to the stresses of reproduction. One study suggests that the rarity of juvenile *T. rex* fossils is due in part to low juvenile mortality rates; the animals were not dying in large numbers at these ages, and thus were not often fossilized. This rarity may also be due to the incompleteness of the [fossil record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_record) or to the bias of fossil collectors towards larger, more spectacular specimens.[^cite_note-ericksonetal2006-117] In a 2013 lecture, Thomas Holtz Jr. suggested that dinosaurs "lived fast and died young" because they reproduced quickly whereas mammals have long lifespans because they take longer to reproduce.[^cite_note-HoltzLecture2013-122] Gregory S. Paul also writes that *Tyrannosaurus* reproduced quickly and died young but attributes their short lifespans to the dangerous lives they lived.[^cite_note-Paul2008Highrisklife-123]

### Skin and possible filamentous feathering

Main article: [Feathered dinosaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feathered_dinosaur)



Fossilized skin impressions from the tail region of a *Tyrannosaurus rex*, [Houston Museum of Natural Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Museum_of_Natural_Science)

The discovery of [feathered dinosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feathered_dinosaur) led to debate regarding whether, and to what extent, *Tyrannosaurus* might have been feathered.[^cite_note-Belletal2017-124][^cite_note-NYT-20190307-125] Filamentous structures, which are commonly recognized as the precursors of [feathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feather), have been reported in the small-bodied, basal tyrannosauroid *[Dilong paradoxus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilong_paradoxus)* from the Early Cretaceous [Yixian Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yixian_Formation) of China in 2004.[^cite_note-xuetal2004-126] Because [integumentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integument) impressions of larger tyrannosauroids known at that time showed evidence of [scales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scale_\(anatomy\)), the researchers who studied *Dilong* speculated that insulating feathers might have been lost by larger species due to their smaller surface-to-volume ratio.[^cite_note-xuetal2004-126] The subsequent discovery of the giant species *[Yutyrannus huali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yutyrannus_huali)*, also from the Yixian, showed that even some large tyrannosauroids had feathers covering much of their bodies, casting doubt on the hypothesis that they were a size-related feature.[^cite_note-yutyrannus-127] A 2017 study reviewed known skin impressions of tyrannosaurids, including those of a *Tyrannosaurus* specimen nicknamed "Wyrex" (HMNS 2006.1743.01, formerly known as BHI 6230) which preserves patches of mosaic scales on the tail, hip, and neck.[^cite_note-Belletal2017-124] The study concluded that feather covering of large tyrannosaurids such as *Tyrannosaurus* was, if present, limited to the upper side of the trunk.[^cite_note-Belletal2017-124]

A conference abstract published in 2016 posited that theropods such as *Tyrannosaurus* had their upper teeth covered in lips, instead of bare teeth as seen in [crocodilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocodilia). This was based on the presence of [enamel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tooth_enamel), which according to the study needs to remain hydrated, an issue not faced by aquatic animals like crocodilians.[^cite_note-\:0-55] However, there has been criticism where it favors the idea for lips, with the 2017 analytical study proposing that tyrannosaurids had large, flat scales on their snouts instead of lips, as modern crocodiles do.[^cite_note-carr2017-53][^cite_note-naish2018-128] But crocodiles possess rather cracked keratinized skin, not flat scales; by observing the hummocky rugosity of tyrannosaurids, and comparing it to extant lizards, researchers have found that tyrannosaurids had squamose scales rather than a crocodillian-like skin.[^cite_note-MontevialeCroc-56][^cite_note-129]

In 2023, Cullen and colleagues supported the idea that theropods like tyrannosaurids had lips based on anatomical patterns, such as those of the foramina on their face and jaws, more similar to those of modern [squamates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squamate) such as [monitor lizards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monitor_lizard) or [marine iguanas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_iguana) than those of modern [crocodilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocodilia) like [alligators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alligator). Comparison of the teeth of *Daspletosaurus* and [American alligators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_alligator) shows that the enamel of tyrannosaurids had no significant wear and that the teeth of modern crocodilians were eroded on the labial side and were substantially worn. This suggests that it is likely that theropod teeth were kept wet by lips. On the basis of the relationship between hydration and wear resistance, the authors argued that it is unlikely that the teeth of theropods, including tyrannosaurids, would have remained unworn when exposed for a long time, because it would have been hard to maintain hydration. The authors also performed regression analyses to demonstrate the relationship between tooth height and skull length, and found that [varanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varanid) like the [crocodile monitor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocodile_monitor) had substantially greater ratios of tooth height to skull length than those of *Tyrannosaurus*, indicating that the teeth of theropods were not too big to be covered by extraoral tissues when the mouth was closed.[^cite_note-CullenEtAl23-57]

### Sexual dimorphism



Skeleton casts mounted in a mating position, [Jurassic Museum of Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Museum_of_Asturias)

As the number of known specimens increased, scientists began to analyze the variation between individuals and discovered what appeared to be two distinct body types, or *morphs*, similar to some other theropod species. As one of these morphs was more solidly built, it was termed the 'robust' morph while the other was termed '[gracile](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/gracile)'. Several [morphological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphology_\(biology\)) differences associated with the two morphs were used to analyze [sexual dimorphism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_dimorphism) in *T. rex*, with the 'robust' morph usually suggested to be female. For example, the [pelvis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelvis) of several 'robust' specimens seemed to be wider, perhaps to allow the passage of [eggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egg_\(biology\)).[^cite_note-carpenter1990-130] It was also thought that the 'robust' morphology correlated with a reduced [chevron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_\(anatomy\)) on the first tail vertebra, also ostensibly to allow eggs to pass out of the [reproductive tract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_system), as had been erroneously reported for [crocodiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocodile).[^cite_note-larson1994-131]

In recent years, evidence for sexual dimorphism has been weakened. A 2005 study reported that previous claims of sexual dimorphism in crocodile chevron anatomy were in error, casting doubt on the existence of similar dimorphism between *T. rex* sexes.[^cite_note-ericksonetal2005-132] A full-sized chevron was discovered on the first tail vertebra of Sue, an extremely robust individual, indicating that this feature could not be used to differentiate the two morphs anyway. As *T. rex* specimens have been found from [Saskatchewan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saskatchewan) to [New Mexico](21649.md), differences between individuals may be indicative of geographic variation rather than sexual dimorphism. The differences could also be age-related, with 'robust' individuals being older animals.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10]

Only a single *Tyrannosaurus* specimen has been conclusively shown to belong to a specific sex. Examination of B-rex demonstrated the preservation of [soft tissue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_tissue) within several bones. Some of this tissue has been identified as a medullary tissue, a specialized tissue grown only in modern birds as a source of [calcium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium) for the production of [eggshell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eggshell) during [ovulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovulation). As only female birds lay eggs, medullary tissue is only found naturally in females, although males are capable of producing it when injected with female reproductive [hormones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hormone) like [estrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estrogen). This strongly suggests that B-rex was female and that she died during ovulation.[^cite_note-schweitzeretal2005-114] Recent research has shown that medullary tissue is never found in [crocodilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocodilian), which are thought to be the closest living relatives of dinosaurs. The shared presence of medullary tissue in birds and other theropod dinosaurs is further evidence of the close [evolutionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution) relationship between the two.[^cite_note-schweitzeretal2007-133]

### Posture



Outdated reconstruction (by [Charles R. Knight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_R._Knight)), showing upright pose

Like many [bipedal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipedal) dinosaurs, *T. rex* was historically depicted as a 'living tripod', with the body at 45 degrees or less from the vertical and the tail dragging along the ground, similar to a [kangaroo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangaroo). This concept dates from [Joseph Leidy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Leidy)'s 1865 reconstruction of *[Hadrosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrosaurus)*, the first to depict a dinosaur in a bipedal posture.[^cite_note-leidy1865-134] In 1915, convinced that the creature stood upright, [Henry Fairfield Osborn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Fairfield_Osborn), former president of the American Museum of Natural History, further reinforced the notion in unveiling the first complete *T. rex* skeleton arranged this way. It stood in an upright pose for 77 years, until it was dismantled in 1992.[^cite_note-amnhsite-135]

By 1970, scientists realized this pose was incorrect and could not have been maintained by a living animal, as it would have resulted in the [dislocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dislocation_\(medicine\)) or weakening of several [joints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint), including the hips and the articulation between the head and the [spinal column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinal_column).[^cite_note-newman1970-136] The inaccurate AMNH mount inspired similar depictions in many films and paintings (such as [Rudolph Zallinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolph_F._Zallinger)'s famous mural *[The Age of Reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Age_of_Reptiles)* in [Yale University](34273.md)'s [Peabody Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peabody_Museum_of_Natural_History))[^cite_note-137] until the 1990s, when films such as *[Jurassic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park_\(film\))* introduced a more accurate posture to the general public.[^cite_note-138] Modern representations in museums, art, and film show *T. rex* with its body approximately parallel to the ground with the tail extended behind the body to balance the head.[^cite_note-139]

To sit down, *Tyrannosaurus* may have settled its weight backwards and rested its weight on a pubic boot, the wide expansion at the end of the pubis in some dinosaurs. With its weight rested on the pelvis, it may have been free to move the hindlimbs. Getting back up again might have involved some stabilization from the diminutive forelimbs.[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-newman1970-136] The latter known as Newman's pushup theory has been debated. Nonetheless, *Tyrannosaurus* was probably able to get up if it fell, which only would have required placing the limbs below the center of gravity, with the tail as an effective counterbalance. Healed stress fractures in the forelimbs have been put forward both as evidence that the arms cannot have been very useful[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-Padian_(2022)-142] and as evidence that they were indeed used and acquired wounds,[^cite_note-Stevens_et.al.2008-143] like the rest of the body.

### Arms



The forelimbs might have been used to help *T. rex* rise from a resting pose, as seen in this cast ([Bucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bucky_\(Tyrannosaurus_rex\)) specimen)

When *T. rex* was first discovered, the [humerus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humerus) was the only element of the forelimb known.[^cite_note-osborn1905-12] For the initial mounted skeleton as seen by the public in 1915, Osborn substituted longer, three-fingered forelimbs like those of *[Allosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allosaurus)*.[^cite_note-osborn1917-7] A year earlier, [Lawrence Lambe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Lambe) described the short, two-fingered forelimbs of the closely related *Gorgosaurus*.[^cite_note-lambe1914-144] This strongly suggested that *T. rex* had similar forelimbs, but this [hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothesis) was not confirmed until the first complete *T. rex* forelimbs were identified in 1989, belonging to MOR 555 (the "Wankel rex").[^cite_note-hornerlessem1993-145][^cite_note-146] The remains of Sue also include complete forelimbs.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10] *T. rex* arms are very small relative to overall body size, measuring only 1 meter (3.3 ft) long, and some scholars have labelled them as [vestigial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vestigial_organs). However, the bones show large areas for [muscle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscle) attachment, indicating considerable strength. This was recognized as early as 1906 by Osborn, who speculated that the forelimbs may have been used to grasp a mate during [copulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copulation_\(zoology\)).[^cite_note-osborn1906-14] Newman (1970) suggested that the forelimbs were used to assist *Tyrannosaurus* in rising from a prone position.[^cite_note-newman1970-136] Since then, other functions have been proposed, although some scholars find them implausible.[^cite_note-Padian_(2022)-142] [Padian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Padian) (2022) argued that the reduction of the arms in tyrannosaurids did not serve a particular function but was a secondary adaptation, stating that as tyrannosaurids developed larger and more powerful skulls and jaws, the arms got smaller to avoid being bitten or torn by other individuals, particularly during group feedings.[^cite_note-Padian_(2022)-142]



Diagram illustrating arm anatomy

Another possibility is that the forelimbs held struggling prey while it was killed by the tyrannosaur's enormous jaws. This hypothesis may be supported by [biomechanical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomechanics) analysis. *T. rex* forelimb bones exhibit extremely thick [cortical bone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone#Structure), which has been interpreted as evidence that they were developed to withstand heavy loads. The [biceps brachii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biceps_brachii) muscle of an adult *T. rex* was capable of lifting 199 kilograms (439 lb) by itself; other muscles such as the [brachialis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brachialis) would work along with the biceps to make elbow flexion even more powerful. The [M. biceps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M._biceps) muscle of *T. rex* was 3.5 times as powerful as the [human equivalent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_equivalent). A *T. rex* forearm had a limited range of motion, with the shoulder and elbow joints allowing only 40 and 45 degrees of motion, respectively. In contrast, the same two joints in *[Deinonychus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deinonychus)* allow up to 88 and 130 degrees of motion, respectively, while a human arm can rotate 360 degrees at the shoulder and move through 165 degrees at the elbow. The heavy build of the arm bones, strength of the muscles, and limited range of motion may indicate a system evolved to hold fast despite the stresses of a struggling prey animal. In the first detailed scientific description of *Tyrannosaurus* forelimbs, paleontologists Kenneth Carpenter and Matt Smith dismissed notions that the forelimbs were useless or that *Tyrannosaurus* was an obligate scavenger.[^cite_note-carpentersmith2001-147]

The idea that the arms served as weapons when hunting prey have also been proposed by [Steven M.
