Stanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_M._Stanley), who suggested that the arms were used for slashing prey, especially by using the claws to rapidly inflict long, deep gashes to its prey.[^cite_note-148] This was dismissed by Padian, who argued that Stanley based his conclusion on incorrectly estimated forelimb size and range of motion.[^cite_note-Padian_(2022)-142]

### Thermoregulation

Main article: [Physiology of dinosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiology_of_dinosaurs)



Restoration showing partial feathering

*Tyrannosaurus*, like most dinosaurs, was long thought to have an [ectothermic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ectotherm) ("cold-blooded") reptilian [metabolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metabolism). The idea of dinosaur ectothermy was challenged by scientists like [Robert T. Bakker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_T._Bakker) and [John Ostrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ostrom) in the early years of the "[dinosaur renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_renaissance)", beginning in the late 1960s.[^cite_note-bakker1968-149][^cite_note-bakker1972-150] *T. rex* itself was claimed to have been [endothermic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warm-blooded) ("warm-blooded"), implying a very active lifestyle.[^cite_note-bakker1986-37] Since then, several paleontologists have sought to determine the ability of *Tyrannosaurus* to [regulate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermoregulation) its body temperature. Histological evidence of high growth rates in young *T. rex*, comparable to those of mammals and birds, may support the hypothesis of a high metabolism. Growth curves indicate that, as in mammals and birds, *T. rex* growth was limited mostly to immature animals, rather than the [indeterminate growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indeterminate_growth) seen in most other [vertebrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebrate).[^cite_note-hornerpadian2004-113]

[Oxygen isotope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_oxygen) ratios in fossilized bone are sometimes used to determine the temperature at which the bone was deposited, as the ratio between certain isotopes correlates with temperature. In one specimen, the isotope ratios in bones from different parts of the body indicated a temperature difference of no more than 4 to 5 °C (7 to 9 °F) between the vertebrae of the torso and the [tibia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibia) of the lower leg. This small temperature range between the body core and the extremities was claimed by paleontologist Reese Barrick and [geochemist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geochemistry) William Showers to indicate that *T. rex* maintained a constant internal body temperature ([homeothermy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeotherm)) and that it enjoyed a metabolism somewhere between ectothermic reptiles and endothermic mammals.[^cite_note-barrettshowers1994-151] Other scientists have pointed out that the ratio of oxygen isotopes in the fossils today does not necessarily represent the same ratio in the distant past, and may have been altered during or after fossilization ([diagenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diagenesis)).[^cite_note-truemanetal2003-152] Barrick and Showers have defended their conclusions in subsequent papers, finding similar results in another theropod dinosaur from a different continent and tens of millions of years earlier in time (*[Giganotosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giganotosaurus)*).[^cite_note-barrickshowers1999-153] [Ornithischian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithischia) dinosaurs also showed evidence of homeothermy, while [varanid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varanidae) lizards from the same formation did not.[^cite_note-barrickstevens1997-154] In 2022, Wiemann and colleagues used a different approach—the [spectroscopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectroscopy) of lipoxidation signals, which are byproducts of [oxidative phosphorylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxidative_phosphorylation) and correlate with metabolic rates—to show that various dinosaur genera including *Tyrannosaurus* had endothermic metabolisms, on par with that of modern birds and higher than that of mammals. They also suggested that such a metabolism was ancestrally common to all dinosaurs.[^cite_note-wiemann2022-155]

Even if *T. rex* does exhibit evidence of homeothermy, it does not necessarily mean that it was endothermic. Such thermoregulation may also be explained by [gigantothermy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gigantothermy), as in some living [sea turtles](142905.md).[^cite_note-paladinoetal1997-156][^cite_note-chinsamyhillenius2004-157][^cite_note-158] Similar to contemporary crocodilians, openings (dorsotemporal fenestrae) in the skull roofs of *Tyrannosaurus* may have aided thermoregulation.[^cite_note-holliday2019-159] Isotopic analysis found *T. rex* had a body temperature of 36.3 °C (97.3 °F) ± 2.5 °C, suggesting it had an endothermic metabolism. Its endothermic metabolism suggests it could have inhabited cooler, higher latitude environments.[^cite_note-160]

### Soft tissue



*T. rex* femur (MOR 1125) from which demineralized matrix and [peptides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peptides) (insets) were obtained

In the March 2005 issue of *[Science](193513.md)*, [Mary Higby Schweitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Higby_Schweitzer) of [North Carolina State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_State_University) and colleagues announced the recovery of soft tissue from the marrow cavity of a fossilized leg bone from a *T. rex*. The bone had been intentionally, though reluctantly, broken for shipping and then not preserved in the normal manner, specifically because Schweitzer was hoping to test it for soft tissue.[^cite_note-smithsonian-fields-161] Designated as the Museum of the Rockies specimen 1125, or MOR 1125, the dinosaur was previously excavated from the [Hell Creek Formation](3551034.md). Flexible, bifurcating [blood vessels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_vessel) and fibrous but elastic [bone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone) matrix tissue were recognized. In addition, microstructures resembling [blood cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_cell) were found inside the matrix and vessels. The structures bear resemblance to [ostrich](985834.md) blood cells and vessels. Whether an unknown process, distinct from normal fossilization, preserved the material, or the material is original, the researchers do not know, and they are careful not to make any claims about preservation.[^cite_note-MHSetalb-162] If it is found to be original material, any surviving proteins may be used as a means of indirectly guessing some of the DNA content of the dinosaurs involved, because each protein is typically created by a specific gene. The absence of previous finds may be the result of people assuming preserved tissue was impossible, therefore not looking. Since the first, two more tyrannosaurs and a hadrosaur have also been found to have such tissue-like structures.[^cite_note-smithsonian-fields-161] Research on some of the tissues involved has suggested that birds are closer relatives to tyrannosaurs than other modern animals.[^cite_note-163] The original endogenous chemistry was also found in MOR 1125 based on preservation of elements associated with bone remodeling and redeposition (sulfur, calcium, zinc), which showed that the bone cortices are similar to those of extant birds.[^cite_note-164]

In studies reported in *Science* in April 2007, Asara and colleagues concluded that seven traces of [collagen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collagen) proteins detected in purified *T. rex* bone most closely match those reported in [chickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken), followed by frogs and newts. The discovery of proteins from a creature tens of millions of years old, along with similar traces the team found in a mastodon bone at least 160,000 years old, upends the conventional view of fossils and may shift paleontologists' focus from bone hunting to biochemistry. Until these finds, most scientists presumed that fossilization replaced all living tissue with inert minerals. Paleontologist Hans Larsson of McGill University in Montreal, who was not part of the studies, called the finds "a milestone", and suggested that dinosaurs could "enter the field of molecular biology and really slingshot paleontology into the modern world".[^cite_note-165]

The presumed soft tissue was called into question by Thomas Kaye of the [University of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington) and his co-authors in 2008. They contend that what was really inside the tyrannosaur bone was slimy [biofilm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofilm) created by bacteria that coated the voids once occupied by blood vessels and cells.[^cite_note-166] The researchers found that what previously had been identified as remnants of blood cells, because of the presence of iron, were actually [framboids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Framboid), microscopic mineral spheres bearing iron. They found similar spheres in a variety of other fossils from various periods, including an [ammonite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonite). In the ammonite, they found the spheres in a place where the iron they contain could not have had any relationship to the presence of blood.[^cite_note-167] Schweitzer has strongly criticized Kaye's claims and argues that there is no reported evidence that biofilms can produce branching, hollow tubes like those noted in her study.[^cite_note-168] San Antonio, Schweitzer and colleagues published an analysis in 2011 of what parts of the collagen had been recovered, finding that it was the inner parts of the collagen coil that had been preserved, as would have been expected from a long period of protein degradation.[^cite_note-169] Other research challenges the identification of soft tissue as biofilm and confirms finding "branching, vessel-like structures" from within fossilized bone.[^cite_note-170]

### Speed

&#x20;

[Femur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femur) (thigh bone)

[Tibia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibia) (shin bone)

[Metatarsals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metatarsal) (foot bones)

[Dewclaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dewclaw)

[Phalanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phalanges) (toe bones)



Skeletal anatomy of a *T. rex* right leg

Scientists have produced a wide range of possible maximum running speeds for *Tyrannosaurus*: mostly around 9 meters per second (32 km/h; 20 mph), but as low as 4.5–6.8 meters per second (16–24 km/h; 10–15 mph) and as high as 20 meters per second (72 km/h; 45 mph). According to John Hutchinson and colleagues, *Tyrannosaurus* was unlikely to run fast .[^cite_note-HutchinsonGarcia2002TrexSlow-171]

A 2002 report used a mathematical model (validated by applying it to three living animals: [alligators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alligator), [chickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken), and [humans](682482.md); and eight more species, including emus and ostriches[^cite_note-Hutchinson2004-172]) to gauge the leg muscle mass needed for fast running (over 40 km/h or 25 mph).[^cite_note-HutchinsonGarcia2002TrexSlow-171] Scientists who think that *Tyrannosaurus* was able to run point out that hollow bones and other features that would have lightened its body may have kept adult weight to a mere 4.5 metric tons (5.0 short tons) or so, or that other animals like [ostriches](985834.md) and [horses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse) with long, flexible legs are able to achieve high speeds through slower but longer strides.[^cite_note-Hutchinson2004-172] Proposed top speeds exceeded 40 kilometers per hour (25 mph) for *Tyrannosaurus*, but were deemed infeasible because they would require exceptional leg muscles of approximately 40–86% of total body mass. Even moderately fast speeds would have required large leg muscles. If the muscle mass was less, only 18 kilometers per hour (11 mph) for walking or jogging would have been possible.[^cite_note-HutchinsonGarcia2002TrexSlow-171] Holtz noted that tyrannosaurids and some closely related groups had significantly longer [distal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distal) hindlimb components (shin plus foot plus toes) relative to the femur length than most other theropods, and that tyrannosaurids and their close relatives had a tightly interlocked [metatarsus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metatarsus) (foot bones).[^cite_note-Holtz1998TaxonomyCoelurosauria-173] The third metatarsal was squeezed between the second and fourth metatarsals to form a single unit called an [arctometatarsus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctometatarsal). This ankle feature may have helped the animal to run more efficiently.[^cite_note-174] Together, these leg features allowed *Tyrannosaurus* to transmit locomotory forces from the foot to the lower leg more effectively than in earlier theropods.[^cite_note-Holtz1998TaxonomyCoelurosauria-173]



Only known tyrannosaurid trackway (*[Bellatoripes fredlundi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellatoripes_fredlundi)*), from the [Wapiti Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wapiti_Formation), [British Columbia](3392.md)

Additionally, a 2020 study indicates that *Tyrannosaurus* and other tyrannosaurids were exceptionally efficient walkers. Studies by Dececchi et al., compared the leg proportions, body mass, and the gaits of more than 70 species of theropod dinosaurs including *Tyrannosaurus* and its relatives. The research team then applied a variety of methods to estimate each dinosaur's top speed when running as well as how much energy each dinosaur expended while moving at more relaxed speeds such as when walking. Among smaller to medium-sized species such as dromaeosaurids, longer legs appear to be an adaptation for faster running, in line with previous results by other researchers. But for theropods weighing over 1,000 kg (2,200 lb), top running speed is limited by body size, so longer legs instead were found to have correlated with low-energy walking. The results further indicate that smaller theropods evolved long legs as a means to both aid in hunting and escape from larger predators while larger theropods that evolved long legs did so to reduce the energy costs and increase foraging efficiency, as they were freed from the demands of predation pressure due to their role as apex predators. Compared to more basal groups of theropods in the study, tyrannosaurs like *Tyrannosaurus* itself showed a marked increase in foraging efficiency due to reduced energy expenditures during hunting or scavenging. This in turn likely resulted in tyrannosaurs having a reduced need for hunting forays and requiring less food to sustain themselves as a result. Additionally, the research, in conjunction with studies that show tyrannosaurs were more agile than other large-bodied theropods, indicates they were quite well-adapted to a long-distance stalking approach followed by a quick burst of speed to go for the kill. Analogies can be noted between tyrannosaurids and modern wolves as a result, supported by evidence that at least some tyrannosaurids were hunting in group settings.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176]

A study published in 2021 by Pasha van Bijlert et al., calculated the [preferred walking speed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preferred_walking_speed) of *Tyrannosaurus*, reporting a speed of 1.28 meters per second (4.6 km/h; 2.9 mph). While walking, animals reduce their [energy expenditure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cost_of_transport) by choosing certain step rhythms at which their body parts [resonate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resonate). The same would have been true for [dinosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaurs), but previous studies did not fully account for the impact the tail had on their walking speeds. According to the authors, when a dinosaur walked, its tail would slightly sway up and down with each step as a result of the [interspinous ligaments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interspinous_ligaments) suspending the tail. Like rubber bands, these ligaments stored energy when they are stretched due to the swaying of the tail. Using a 3-D model of *Tyrannosaurus* specimen [Trix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trix_\(dinosaur\)), muscles and ligaments were reconstructed to simulate the tail movements. This results in a rhythmic, energy-efficient walking speed for *Tyrannosaurus* similar to that seen in living animals such as humans, ostriches and giraffes.[^cite_note-vanBijlert2021-177]

A 2017 study estimated the top running speed of *Tyrannosaurus* as 17 mph (27 km/h), speculating that *Tyrannosaurus* exhausted its energy reserves long before reaching top speed, resulting in a parabola-like relationship between size and speed.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] Another 2017 study hypothesized that an adult *Tyrannosaurus* was incapable of running due to high skeletal loads. Using a calculated weight estimate of 7 tons, the model showed that speeds above 11 mph (18 km/h) would have probably shattered the leg bones of *Tyrannosaurus*. The finding may mean that running was also not possible for other giant theropod dinosaurs like *[Giganotosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giganotosaurus)*, *[Mapusaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mapusaurus)* and *[Acrocanthosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acrocanthosaurus)*.[^cite_note-180] However, studies by Eric Snively and colleague&#x73;*,* published in 2019 indicate that *Tyrannosaurus* and other tyrannosaurids were more maneuverable than allosauroids and other theropods of comparable size due to low rotational inertia compared to their body mass combined with large leg muscles. As a result, it is hypothesized that *Tyrannosaurus* was capable of making relatively quick turns and could likely pivot its body more quickly when close to its prey, or that while turning, the theropod could "pirouette" on a single planted foot while the alternating leg was held out in a suspended swing during a pursuit. The results of this study potentially could shed light on how agility could have contributed to the success of tyrannosaurid evolution.[^cite_note-181] In 2026, Boeye and colleagues analyzed the foot biomechanics of *T. rex* and determined that its foot likely functioned similarly to modern birds. This may have resulted in shorter stride lengths, higher stride frequencies, and a somewhat elevated top speed.[^cite_note-Boeye2026-18]

### Possible footprints



Probable footprint from [New Mexico](21649.md)

Two isolated fossilized [footprints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Footprint) have been tentatively assigned to *T. rex*. The first was discovered at [Philmont Scout Ranch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philmont_Scout_Ranch), New Mexico, in 1983 by American geologist Charles Pillmore. Originally thought to belong to a [hadrosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrosaurid), examination of the footprint revealed a large 'heel' unknown in [ornithopod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithopod) dinosaur tracks, and traces of what may have been a [hallux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hallux), the dewclaw-like fourth digit of the tyrannosaur foot. The footprint was published as the [ichnogenus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ichnogenus) *[Tyrannosauripus pillmorei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosauripus_pillmorei)* in 1994, by [Martin Lockley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Lockley) and Adrian Hunt. Lockley and Hunt suggested that it was very likely the track was made by a *T. rex*, which would make it the first known footprint from this species. The track was made in what was once a vegetated wetland mudflat. It measures 83 centimeters (33 in) long by 71 centimeters (28 in) wide.[^cite_note-lockley\&hunt1994-182]

A second footprint that may have been made by a *Tyrannosaurus* was first reported in 2007 by British paleontologist Phil Manning, from the [Hell Creek Formation](3551034.md) of Montana. This second track measures 72 centimeters (28 in) long, shorter than the track described by Lockley and Hunt. Whether or not the track was made by *Tyrannosaurus* is unclear, though *Tyrannosaurus* is the only large theropod known to have existed in the Hell Creek Formation.[^cite_note-rextrack2007-183][^cite_note-manningetal2008-184]

A set of footprints in Glenrock, Wyoming dating to the [Maastrichtian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maastrichtian) stage of the Late Cretaceous and hailing from the [Lance Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lance_Formation) were described by Scott Persons, Phil Currie and colleagues in 2016, and are believed to belong to either a juvenile *T. rex* or *Nanotyrannus lancensis*. From measurements and based on the positions of the footprints, the animal was believed to be traveling at a walking speed of around 2.8 to 5 miles per hour and was estimated to have a hip height of 1.56 to 2.06 m (5.1 to 6.8 ft).[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] A follow-up paper appeared in 2017, increasing the speed estimations by 50–80%.[^cite_note-188]

Rare fossil footprints and trackways found in New Mexico and Wyoming that are assigned to the ichnogenus *[Tyrannosauripus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosauripus)* have been attributed to being made by *Tyrannosaurus*, based on the stratigraphic age of the rocks they are preserved in. The first specimen, found in 1994 was described by Lockley and Hunt and consists of a single, large footprint. Another pair of ichnofossils, described in 2021, show a large tyrannosaurid rising from a prone position by rising up using its elbows in conjunction with the pads on their feet to stand. These two unique sets of fossils were found in Ludlow, Colorado and Cimarron, New Mexico.[^cite_note-189] Another ichnofossil described in 2018, perhaps belonging to a juvenile *Tyrannosaurus* or *Nanotyrannus* was uncovered in the Lance Formation of Wyoming. The trackway itself offers a rare glimpse into the walking speed of tyrannosaurids, and the trackmaker is estimated to have been moving at a speed of 4.5–8.0 kilometers per hour (2.8–5.0 mph), significantly faster than previously assumed for estimations of walking speed in tyrannosaurids.[^cite_note-190]

A trackway consisting of four footprints discovered near [Marmarth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmarth,_North_Dakota), North Dakota in 2025 and described in 2026 have been attributed to an adult *Tyrannosaurus* based on the shape and size of the tracks, which measure 1 m (3.3 ft)*.* The set of tracks suggests a stride length of 4 m (13 ft) for the animal that made them, with speed estimates of 1.5–2 meters per second (3.4-4.5 mph). The fluvial environment of the upper Hell Creek Formation, from which the footprints were unearthed may explain the significant scarcity of trackways and the uncommon nature of theropod footprints in the formation in general. Three of the footprints are expected to be recovered in autumn of 2026 to be displayed at the [Denver Museum of Nature and Science](2983589.md).[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]

### Brain and senses



The eye-sockets faced mainly forwards, giving it good [binocular vision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binocular_vision) (reconstructed skull of the [Sue specimen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_specimen); the original was partly crushed and broken).

A study conducted by [Lawrence Witmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Witmer) and Ryan Ridgely of Ohio University found that *Tyrannosaurus* shared the heightened sensory abilities of other [coelurosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coelurosaur), highlighting relatively rapid and coordinated eye and head movements; an enhanced ability to sense low frequency sounds, which would allow tyrannosaurs to track prey movements from long distances; and an enhanced sense of smell.[^cite_note-witmer2009-194] A study published by Kent Stevens concluded that *Tyrannosaurus* had keen vision. By applying modified [perimetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perimetry) to facial reconstructions of several dinosaurs including *Tyrannosaurus*, the study found that *Tyrannosaurus* had a binocular range of 55 degrees, surpassing that of modern hawks. Stevens estimated that *Tyrannosaurus* had 13 times the visual acuity of a human and surpassed the visual acuity of an eagle, which is 3.6 times that of a person. Stevens estimated a limiting far point (that is, the distance at which an object can be seen as separate from the horizon) as far as 6 km (3.7 mi) away, which is greater than the 1.6 km (1 mi) that a human can see.[^cite_note-Stevens2006Binocular-44][^cite_note-jaffe-45][^cite_note-stevenswebpage-195]

Thomas Holtz Jr. would note that high depth perception of *Tyrannosaurus* may have been due to the prey it had to hunt, noting that it had to hunt ceratopsians such as *[Triceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops)*, ankylosaurs such as *[Ankylosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankylosaurus)*, and hadrosaurs. He would suggest that this made precision more crucial for *Tyrannosaurus* enabling it to, "get in, get that blow in and take it down." In contrast, *[Acrocanthosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acrocanthosaurus)* had limited depth perception because they hunted large sauropods, which were relatively rare during the time of *Tyrannosaurus*.[^cite_note-HoltzLecture2013-122]

Though no *Tyrannosaurus* [scleral ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scleral_ring) has been found, [Kenneth Carpenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_Carpenter) estimated its size based on that of *Gorgosaurus*. The inferred scleral ring for the Stan specimen is \~7 cm (2.8 in) in diameter with an internal aperture diameter of \~3.5 cm (1.4 in). Based on eye proportions in living reptiles, this implies a pupil diameter of about 2.5 cm (0.98 in), an iris diameter about that of the scleral ring, and an eyeball diameter of 11–12 cm (4.3–4.7 in). Carpenter also estimated an eyeball depth of \~7.7–9.6 cm (3.0–3.8 in). Based on these calculations, the [f-number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-number) for Stan's eye is 3–3.8; since [diurnal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diurnality) animals have f-numbers of 2.1 or higher, this would indicate that *Tyrannosaurus* had poor low-light vision and hunted during the day.[^cite_note-Trexpaleobiologychapter14-196]

*Tyrannosaurus* had very large [olfactory bulbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olfactory_bulb) and [olfactory nerves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olfactory_nerve) relative to their brain size, the organs responsible for a heightened sense of smell. This suggests that the sense of smell was highly developed, and implies that tyrannosaurs could detect carcasses by scent alone across great distances. The sense of smell in tyrannosaurs may have been comparable to modern [vultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulture), which use scent to track carcasses for scavenging. Research on the olfactory bulbs has shown that *T. rex* had the most highly developed sense of smell of 21 sampled non-avian dinosaur species.[^cite_note-Calgary_Herald-197]



Cast of the braincase at the [Australian Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Museum), Sydney

Somewhat unusually among theropods, *T. rex* had a very long [cochlea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cochlea). The length of the cochlea is often related to hearing acuity, or at least the importance of hearing in behavior, implying that hearing was a particularly important sense to tyrannosaurs. Specifically, data suggests that *T. rex* heard best in the low-frequency range, and that low-frequency sounds were an important part of tyrannosaur behavior.[^cite_note-witmer2009-194] A 2017 study by Thomas Carr and colleagues found that the snout of tyrannosaurids was highly sensitive, based on a high number of small openings in the facial bones of the related *Daspletosaurus* that contained [sensory neurons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sensory_neuron). The study speculated that tyrannosaurs might have used their sensitive snouts to measure the temperature of their nests and to gently pick up eggs and hatchlings, as seen in modern crocodilians.[^cite_note-carr2017-53] Another study published in 2021 further suggests that *Tyrannosaurus* had an acute sense of touch, based on neurovascular canals in the front of its jaws, which it could utilize to better detect and consume prey. The study, published by Kawabe and Hittori et al., suggests that *Tyrannosaurus* could also accurately sense slight differences in material and movement, allowing it to utilize different feeding strategies on different parts of its prey's carcasses depending on the situation. The sensitive neurovascular canals of *Tyrannosaurus* also likely were adapted to performing fine movements and behaviors such as nest building, parental care, and other social behavior such as intraspecific communication. The results of this study also align with results made in studying the related tyrannosaurid *[Daspletosaurus horneri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daspletosaurus_horneri)* and the [allosauroid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allosauroid) *[Neovenator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neovenator)*, which have similar neurovascular adaptations, suggesting that the faces of theropods were highly sensitive to pressure and touch.[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199] However, a more recent study reviewing the evolution of the trigeminal canals among sauropsids notes that a much denser network of neurovascular canals in the snout and lower jaw is more commonly encountered in aquatic or semiaquatic taxa (e.g., *[Spinosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinosaurus)*, *[Halszkaraptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halszkaraptor)*, *[Plesiosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plesiosaurus)*), and taxa that developed a rhamphotheca (e.g., *[Caenagnathasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caenagnathasia)*), while the network of canals in *Tyrannosaurus* appears simpler, though still more derived than in most ornithischians, and overall terrestrial taxa such as tyrannosaurids and *Neovenator* may have had average facial sensitivity for non-edentulous terrestrial theropods, although further research is needed. The neurovascular canals in *Tyrannosaurus* may instead have supported soft tissue structures for thermoregulation or social signaling, the latter of which could be confirmed by the fact that the neurovascular network of canals may have changed during ontogeny.[^cite_note-200]

A study by Grant R. Hurlburt, Ryan C. Ridgely and Lawrence Witmer obtained estimates for [Encephalization Quotients](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encephalization_Quotient) (EQs), based on reptiles and birds, as well as estimates for the ratio of cerebrum to brain mass. The study concluded that *Tyrannosaurus* had the relatively largest brain of all adult non-avian dinosaurs with the exception of certain small maniraptoriforms (*[Bambiraptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bambiraptor)*, *[Troodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troodon)* and *[Ornithomimus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithomimus)*). The study found that *Tyrannosaurus'*&#x73; relative brain size was still within the range of modern reptiles, being at most 2 [standard deviations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_deviations) above the mean of non-avian reptile EQs. The estimates for the ratio of cerebrum mass to brain mass would range from 47.5 to 49.53 percent. According to the study, this is more than the lowest estimates for extant birds (44.6 percent), but still close to the typical ratios of the smallest sexually mature alligators which range from 45.9 to 47.9 percent.[^cite_note-Trexpaleobiologychapter6-201] Other studies, such as those by Steve Brusatte, indicate the encephalization quotient of *Tyrannosaurus* was similar in range (2.0–2.4) to a [chimpanzee](7844.md) (2.2–2.5), though this may be debatable as reptilian and mammalian encephalization quotients are not equivalent.[^cite_note-202]

### Social behavior



Mounted skeletons of different age groups (skeleton in lower left based on *[Stygivenator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stygivenator)*), [Natural History Museum of Los Angeles County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_History_Museum_of_Los_Angeles_County)

[Philip J. Currie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_J._Currie) suggested that Tyrannosaurus may have been [pack hunters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pack_hunter), comparing *T. rex* to related species *[Tarbosaurus bataar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarbosaurus_bataar)* and *[Albertosaurus sarcophagus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albertosaurus_sarcophagus)*, citing fossil evidence that may indicate [gregarious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociality) (describing animals that travel in herds or packs) behavior.[^cite_note-discoverdinogangs-203] A find in [South Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dakota) where three *T. rex* skeletons were in close proximity may suggest the formation of a pack.[^cite_note-tgraphpack-204][^cite_note-djournal-205] Cooperative pack hunting may have been an effective strategy for subduing prey with advanced [anti-predator adaptations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-predator_adaptation) which pose potential lethality such as *[Triceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops)* and *[Ankylosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankylosaurus)*.[^cite_note-discoverdinogangs-203]

Currie's pack-hunting *T. rex* hypothesis has been criticized for not having been [peer-reviewed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer-review), but rather was discussed in a television interview and book called *Dino Gangs*.[^cite_note-switek2011dinogangs-206] The Currie theory for pack hunting by *T. rex* is based mainly by analogy to a different species, *Tarbosaurus bataar*. Evidence of gregariousness in *T. bataar* itself has not been peer-reviewed, and to Currie's own admission, can only be interpreted with reference to evidence in other closely related species. According to Currie gregariousness in *Albertosaurus sarcophagus* is supported by the discovery of 26 individuals with varied ages in the Dry Island bonebed. He ruled out the possibility of a predator trap due to the similar preservation state of individuals and the near absence of herbivores.[^cite_note-switek2011dinogangs-206][^cite_note-currie1998-207]

Additional support of tyrannosaurid gregariousness can be found in fossilized [trackways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trackway) from the Upper Cretaceous [Wapiti Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wapiti_Formation) of northeastern [British Columbia](3392.md), Canada, left by three tyrannosaurids traveling in the same direction.[^cite_note-TG-20140724-208][^cite_note-209] According to scientists assessing the Dino Gangs program, the evidence for pack hunting in *Tarbosaurus* and *Albertosaurus* is weak and based on group skeletal remains for which alternate explanations may apply (such as drought or a flood forcing dinosaurs to die together in one place).[^cite_note-switek2011dinogangs-206] Others researchers have speculated that instead of large theropod social groups, some of these finds represent behavior more akin to [Komodo dragon](17360.md)-like mobbing of carcasses, even going as far as to say true pack-hunting behavior may not exist in any non-avian dinosaurs due to its rarity in modern predators.[^cite_note-roachbrinkman2007-210] Pathologies of *Tyrannosaurus* specimens have been suggested as evidence of conspecific attack, including "Wyrex" with a hole penetrating its jugual and severe trauma on its tail that shows signs of [bone remodeling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone_remodeling) (not regrowth).[^cite_note-Trexpaleobiologychapter10-211][^cite_note-212]

### Diet and feeding behavior

Main article: [Feeding behavior of *Tyrannosaurus*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feeding_behavior_of_Tyrannosaurus)





*Tyrannosaurus* tooth marks on bones of various herbivorous dinosaurs (left) as well as other *Tyrannosaurus* (right)

Most paleontologists have always accepted that *Tyrannosaurus* was an active [predator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predator) and, like most large [carnivores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivores), would also opportunistically [scavenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scavenger).[^cite_note-nationalgeographic-213][^cite_note-Witton_2025-214] By far the largest carnivore in its environment, *T. rex* was most likely an [apex predator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apex_predator), preying upon [hadrosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrosaur), armored herbivores like [ceratopsians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratopsia) and [ankylosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankylosaur), and possibly [sauropods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauropod) (such as *[Alamosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alamosaurus)*).[^cite_note-215] Enamel δ<sup>44/42</sup>Ca values also suggest the possibility that *T. rex* occasionally fed on carcasses of marine reptiles and fish washed up on the shores of the [Western Interior Seaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Interior_Seaway).[^cite_note-216] A study in 2012 by Karl Bates and Peter Falkingham found that *Tyrannosaurus* had the most powerful bite of any terrestrial animal that has ever lived, finding an adult *Tyrannosaurus* could have exerted 35,000 to 57,000 [N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newton_\(unit\)) (7,868 to 12,814 [lbf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pound_\(force\))) of force in the back teeth.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-BatesFalkingham2012-218][^cite_note-219] Even higher estimates were made by Mason B. Meers in 2003.[^cite_note-MM03-47] This allowed it to crush bones during repetitive biting and fully consume the carcasses of large dinosaurs.[^cite_note-gignac-26] Stephan Lautenschlager and colleagues calculated that *Tyrannosaurus* was capable of a maximum jaw gape of around 80 degrees, a necessary adaptation for a wide range of jaw angles to power the creature's strong bite.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221]

A discredited fringe hypothesis that was never seriously considered was that *Tyrannosaurus* was an obligate [scavenger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scavenger). The argument originated in a 1917 study by Lambe which argued that large theropods were pure scavengers because *Gorgosaurus* teeth showed hardly any wear.[^cite_note-Lambe1917Gorgosaurus-222] This argument disregarded the fact that theropods rapidly replaced their teeth. Ever since the first discovery of *Tyrannosaurus* most scientists have agreed that it was an active predator, though like modern large predators it would readily scavenge or steal another predator's kill if it had the opportunity.[^cite_note-FarlowHoltz2002FossilRecordPredation-223][^cite_note-Witton_2025-214]

Paleontologist [Jack Horner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Horner_\(paleontologist\)) has been a major proponent of the view that *Tyrannosaurus* was not a predator at all but instead was an obligate scavenger.[^cite_note-hornerlessem1993-145][^cite_note-Horner1994SteakKnives-224][^cite_note-BBC2003TrexOnTrial-225] He has put forward arguments in the popular literature to support the pure scavenger hypothesis:



A *Tyrannosaurus* mounted next to a *[Triceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops)* at the [Los Angeles Natural History Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Natural_History_Museum)

- Tyrannosaur arms are short when compared to other known predators. Horner argues that the arms were too short to make the necessary gripping force to hold on to prey.

  [^cite_note-BBC2003TrexOnTrial-225]

  &#x20;Other paleontologists such as [Thomas Holtz Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_R._Holtz_Jr.) pointed out that there are plenty of modern-day predators that do not use their forelimbs to hunt such as [wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf), [hyenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyena), and [secretary birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretarybird) as well as other extinct animals thought to be predators that would not have used their forelimbs such as [phorusrhacids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phorusrhacidae).

  [^cite_note-FarlowHoltz2002FossilRecordPredation2-226]

  [^cite_note-Tyrantkingbook-227]

- Tyrannosaurs had large [olfactory bulbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olfactory_bulb) and [olfactory nerves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olfactory_nerve) (relative to their brain size). These suggest a highly developed sense of smell which could sniff out carcasses over great distances, as modern [vultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulture) do. Research on the olfactory bulbs of dinosaurs has shown that *Tyrannosaurus* had the most highly developed sense of smell of 21 sampled dinosaurs.

  [^cite_note-Calgary_Herald-197]

  &#x20;However, good olfactory capabilities are also common in predatory animals, so this fails to provide evidence against predatory behavior.

  [^cite_note-Witton_2025-214]

- Tyrannosaur teeth could crush bone, and therefore could extract as much food ([bone marrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone_marrow)) as possible from carcass remnants, usually the least nutritious parts. Karen Chin and colleagues have found bone fragments in [coprolites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coprolite) (fossilized feces) that they attribute to tyrannosaurs, but point out that a tyrannosaur's teeth were not well adapted to systematically chewing bone like [hyenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyena) do to extract marrow.

  [^cite_note-ChinEtal1998KingSizeCoprolite-228]

- Since at least some of *Tyrannosaurus*'s potential prey could move quickly, evidence that it walked instead of ran could indicate that it was a scavenger.

  [^cite_note-Horner1994SteakKnives-224]

  &#x20;However, most analyses indicate *Tyrannosaurus*, while slower than large modern terrestrial predators due to being far larger, was still faster than large [hadrosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrosaurs) and [ceratopsians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratopsians).

  [^cite_note-HutchinsonGarcia2002TrexSlow-171]

  [^cite_note-manningetal2008-184]

  [^cite_note-Witton_2025-214]

Most evidence suggests hunting behavior in *Tyrannosaurus*. The eye sockets of tyrannosaurs are positioned so that the eyes would point forward, giving them [binocular vision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binocular_vision) slightly better than that of modern [hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawk). It is not obvious why [natural selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_selection) would have favored this long-term trend if tyrannosaurs had been pure scavengers, which would not have needed the advanced [depth perception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depth_perception) that [stereoscopic vision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereoscopic_vision) provides.[^cite_note-Stevens2006Binocular-44][^cite_note-jaffe-45] In modern animals, binocular vision is found mainly in predators (though not all predators have well-developed binocular vision).



The damage to the tail vertebrae of this *Edmontosaurus annectens* skeleton (on display at the Denver Museum of Nature and Science) indicates that it may have been bitten by a *Tyrannosaurus*

There is at least one and possibly two fossil specimens of the hadrosaur *[Edmontosaurus annectens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmontosaurus_annectens)* with healed caudal injuries caused by *Tyrannosaurus* bites made while the herbivore was alive. One pair of *Edmontosaurus* caudal (tail) [vertebrae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebra) has the tip of an adult *Tyrannosaurus* tooth embedded in the bone, with evidence of new bone growth that wrapped around the tooth, showing that this individual had also survived a *Tyrannosaurus* attack.[^cite_note-toothincaudal-229][^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233] Another specimen of *Edmontosaurus* (in some newspapers it was misidentified as *[Hadrosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrosaurus)*) from Montana showed healed supposed tyrannosaur-inflicted damage on its tail vertebrae where some of its caudal neural spines was mutilated;[^cite_note-carpenter1998-234] however, in this case the damage is ambiguous and not directly attributable to *Tyrannosaurus*.[^cite_note-toothincaudal-229] There is also evidence for an aggressive interaction between a *[Triceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops)* and a *Tyrannosaurus* in the form of partially healed tyrannosaur tooth marks on a *Triceratops* brow horn and [squamosal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squamosal) (a bone of the [neck frill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neck_frill)); the bitten horn is also broken, with new bone growth after the break. It is not known what the exact nature of the interaction was, though: either animal could have been the aggressor.[^cite_note-JH08-235] Since the *Triceratops* wounds healed, it is most likely that the *Triceratops* survived the encounter and managed to overcome the *Tyrannosaurus*. In a battle against a bull *Triceratops*, the *Triceratops* would likely defend itself by inflicting fatal wounds to the *Tyrannosaurus* using its sharp horns.[^cite_note-236] Studies of [Sue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_\(dinosaur\)) found a broken and healed [fibula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fibula) and tail vertebrae, scarred facial bones and a tooth from another *Tyrannosaurus* embedded in a neck vertebra, providing evidence for aggressive behavior.[^cite_note-TC98-237]

In 1992, [William Abler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Abler) suggested that *Tyrannosaurus* may have had infectious [saliva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saliva) used to kill its prey. Abler observed that the [serrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_dinosaur_anatomy#serrations) (tiny protuberances) on the cutting edges of the teeth are closely spaced, enclosing little chambers, which he argued trapped pieces of carcass with bacteria, giving *Tyrannosaurus* a deadly, infectious bite, as the [Komodo dragon](17360.md) was mistakenly thought to have.[^cite_note-abler1992-238] Jack Horner and Don Lessem, in a 1993 popular book, questioned Abler's hypothesis, arguing that *Tyrannosaurus*'s tooth serrations as more like cubes in shape than the serrations on a Komodo monitor's teeth, which are rounded.[^cite_note-hornerlessem1993-145]<sup>: 214–215 </sup> The basis for an infectious bite in *Tyrannosaurus* was further undermined when it was found Komodo dragons do not actually have an infectious bite nor hunt by biting prey and tracking it as it weakens.[^cite_note-goldstein2013-239]

*Tyrannosaurus* probably primarily processed carcasses with lateral shakes of the head, like crocodilians. The head was not as maneuverable as the skulls of [allosauroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allosauroidea), due to flat joints of the neck vertebrae.[^cite_note-ESetal2013-240][^cite_note-Witton_2025-214] Studies on hadrosaur vertebrae from the Hell Creek Formation that were punctured by the teeth of what appears to be a late-stage juvenile *Tyrannosaurus* indicate that despite lacking the bone-crushing adaptations of the adults, young individuals were still capable of using the same bone-puncturing feeding technique.[^cite_note-Peterson-241]

One study of *Tyrannosaurus* specimens with tooth marks in the bones attributable to the same genus was presented as evidence of [cannibalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannibalism).[^cite_note-HornerCurrie_et.al._2010_Trex_cannibalism-242] Tooth marks in the [humerus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humerus), foot bones and [metatarsals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metatarsal) may indicate opportunistic scavenging, rather than wounds caused by combat.[^cite_note-HornerCurrie_et.al._2010_Trex_cannibalism-242][^cite_note-243] Other [tyrannosaurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosaurids) may also have practiced cannibalism.[^cite_note-HornerCurrie_et.al._2010_Trex_cannibalism-242]

### Parenting

While there is no direct evidence of *Tyrannosaurus* raising their young (given the rarity of juvenile and nest Tyrannosaur fossils), it has been suggested that like its closest living relatives, birds and crocodiles, *Tyrannosaurus* may have protected and fed its young. Crocodilians and birds are often suggested by some paleontologists to be modern analogues for dinosaur parenting.[^cite_note-244] Direct evidence of parental behavior exists in other dinosaurs such as *[Maiasaura peeblesorum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maiasaura)*, the first dinosaur to have been discovered to raise its young, as well as more closely related [Oviraptorids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oviraptoridae), the latter suggesting parental behavior in theropods.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] Some very young specimens assigned to *Tyrannosaurus rex* suggest that, although the species may have provided parental care, it was likely limited.[^cite_note-Longrich2026-121]

### Pathology





Jaw bones (top) and life restoration showing injuries caused by either parasite infections or bites

In 2001, Bruce Rothschild and others published a study examining evidence for [stress fractures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stress_fractures) and [tendon avulsions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tendon_avulsions) in [theropod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theropod) dinosaurs and the implications for their behavior. Since stress fractures are caused by repeated trauma rather than singular events they are more likely to be caused by regular behavior than other types of injuries. Of the 81 *Tyrannosaurus* foot bones examined in the study, one was found to have a stress fracture, while none of the 10 hand bones were found to have stress fractures. The researchers found tendon avulsions only among *Tyrannosaurus* and *[Allosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allosaurus)*. An avulsion injury left a divot on the humerus of Sue the *T. rex*, apparently located at the origin of the [deltoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deltoid_muscle) or [teres major](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teres_major) muscles. The presence of avulsion injuries being limited to the forelimb and shoulder in both *Tyrannosaurus* and *Allosaurus* suggests that theropods may have had a musculature more complex than and functionally different from those of birds. The researchers concluded that Sue's tendon avulsion was probably obtained from struggling prey. The presence of stress fractures and tendon avulsions, in general, provides evidence for a "very active" predation-based diet rather than obligate scavenging.[^cite_note-rothschild-dino-250]

A 2009 study showed that smooth-edged holes in the skulls of several specimens might have been caused by *[Trichomonas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trichomonas)*-like parasites that commonly infect [birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird). According to the study, seriously infected individuals, including "Sue" and MOR 980 ("Peck's Rex"), might therefore have died from starvation after feeding became increasingly difficult. Previously, these holes had been explained by the bacterious bone infection [Actinomycosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Actinomycosis) or by intraspecific attacks.[^cite_note-Wolff-251] A subsequent study found that while trichomoniasis has many attributes of the model proposed, the observed sharp margins with little reactive bone shown by the radiographs of *Trichomonas*-infected birds are dissimilar to the reactive bone seen in the affected *T. rex* specimens. Also, trichomoniasis can be very rapidly fatal in birds (14 days or less) albeit in its milder form, and this suggests that if a *Trichomonas*-like protozoan is the culprit, trichomoniasis was less acute in its non-avian dinosaur form during the Late Cretaceous. Finally, the relative size of this type of lesions is much larger in small bird throats, and may not have been enough to choke a *T. rex*.[^cite_note-252] A more recent study examining the pathologies concluded that the osseous alteration observed most closely resembles those around healing human cranial trepanations and healing fractures in the Triassic reptile *[Stagonolepis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stagonolepis),* in the absence of infection. The possible cause may instead have been intraspecific combat.[^cite_note-253]

## Paleoecology



Fauna of Hell Creek (*Tyrannosaurus* in dark red, left).

*Tyrannosaurus* lived during what is referred to as the [Lancian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancian) faunal stage ([Maastrichtian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maastrichtian) age) at the end of the Late Cretaceous. *Tyrannosaurus* ranged from [Canada](5042916.md) in the north to at least New Mexico in the south of [Laramidia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laramidia).[^cite_note-larson2008-8] Several notable *Tyrannosaurus* remains have been found in the [Hell Creek Formation](3551034.md). During the Maastrichtian this area was [maritime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) to [subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropical), with a warm and humid climate.[^cite_note-254] The flora consisted mostly of [angiosperms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angiosperms), but also included trees like dawn redwood (*[Metasequoia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metasequoia)*).[^cite_note-255] Archosaurs discovered from this formation include the [ceratopsians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratopsia) *[Leptoceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leptoceratops)*, *[Torosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torosaurus)*, and *[Triceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops)*,[^cite_note-256] the hadrosaurid *[Edmontosaurus annectens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmontosaurus_annectens)*,[^cite_note-257] the [parksosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parksosauridae) *[Thescelosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thescelosaurus)*,[^cite_note-258] the [ankylosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankylosaur) *[Ankylosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankylosaurus)* and *[Denversaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denversaurus)*,[^cite_note-259] the [pachycephalosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pachycephalosaur) *[Pachycephalosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pachycephalosaurus)*, *[Platytholus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platytholus)* and *[Sphaerotholus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphaerotholus)*,[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261] the [paravian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraves) theropods *[Acheroraptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acheroraptor)* and *[Pectinodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pectinodon)*,[^cite_note-262] other [maniraptoran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maniraptora) theropods *[Anzu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anzu_wyliei)*, *[Eoneophron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eoneophron)*, *[Trierarchuncus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trierarchuncus)* and indeterminate [ornithomimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithomimid),[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264] the [tyrannosauroid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosauroid) *[Nanotyrannus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanotyrannus)*,[^cite_note-Z\&N25-24] and the [azhdarchid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azhdarchid) pterosaur *[Infernodrakon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infernodrakon)*.[^cite_note-265]



*Tyrannosaurus* and other animals of the Hell Creek Formation

Another formation with *Tyrannosaurus* remains is the [Lance Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lance_Formation) of Wyoming. This has been interpreted as a [bayou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayou) environment similar to today's Gulf Coast. The fauna was very similar to Hell Creek, but with *[Struthiomimus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Struthiomimus)* replacing its relative *Ornithomimus*. The small ceratopsian *[Leptoceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leptoceratops)* also lived in the area.[^cite_note-266]

In its southern range, specifically based on remains discovered from the [North Horn Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Horn_Formation) of [Utah](31716.md), *Tyrannosaurus rex* lived alongside the [titanosaur](1139347.md) *[Utetitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utetitan)*, the [ceratopsid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratopsidae) *[Torosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torosaurus)* and the indeterminate [troodontids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troodontidae) and [hadrosaurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrosauridae).[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268][^cite_note-Utetitan-269] *Tyrannosaurus mcraeensis* from the [McRae Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McRae_Group) of [New Mexico](21649.md) coexisted with the [ceratopsid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratopsidae) *[Sierraceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierraceratops)* and possibly the titanosaur *Alamosaurus*.[^cite_note-T.mcraeensis-66] Potential remains identified as cf. *Tyrannosaurus* have also been discovered from the [Javelina Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javelina_Formation) of [Texas](29810.md),[^cite_note-T.mcraeensis-66] where the remains of the titanosaur (either *Alamosaurus* or *Utetitan*),[^cite_note-Utetitan-269] the ceratopsid *[Bravoceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bravoceratops)*, the [pterosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pterosaur) *[Quetzalcoatlus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quetzalcoatlus)* and *[Wellnhopterus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellnhopterus)*, and possible species of troodontids and hadrosaurids are found.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-wellnhopterus-271][^cite_note-272] Its southern range is thought to have been dominated by semi-arid inland plains, following the probable retreat of the [Western Interior Seaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Interior_Seaway) as global sea levels fell.[^cite_note-273]

*Tyrannosaurus* may have also inhabited Mexico's [Lomas Coloradas Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lomas_Coloradas_Formation) in Sonora. Though skeletal evidence is lacking, six shed and broken teeth from the fossil bed have been thoroughly compared with other theropod genera and appear to be identical to those of *Tyrannosaurus*. If true, the evidence indicates the range of *Tyrannosaurus* was possibly more extensive than previously believed.[^cite_note-274] It is possible that tyrannosaurs were originally Asian species, migrating to North America before the end of the Cretaceous period.[^cite_note-275]

### Population estimates



Chart of the time-averaged census for large-bodied dinosaurs from the entire Hell Creek Formation in the study area

According to studies published in 2021 by Charles Marshall et al., the total population of adult *Tyrannosaurus* at any given time was perhaps 20,000 individuals, with computer estimations also suggesting a total population no lower than 1,300 and no higher than 328,000. The authors themselves suggest that the estimate of 20,000 individuals is probably lower than what should be expected, especially when factoring in that disease pandemics could easily wipe out such a small population. Over the span of the genus' existence, it is estimated that there were about 127,000 generations, totalling roughly 2.5 billion animals.[^cite_note-nytimes2021-276][^cite_note-Marshall2021-277]

In the same paper, it is suggested that in a population of *Tyrannosaurus* adults numbering 20,000, the number of individuals living in an area the size of California could be as high as 3,800 animals, while an area the size of Washington D.C. could support a population of only two adult *Tyrannosaurus*. The study does not take into account the number of juvenile animals present due to their occupation of a different niche than the adults, and thus it is likely the total population was much higher. Simultaneously, studies of living carnivores suggest that some predator populations are higher in density than others of similar weight (such as jaguars and hyenas, which have vastly differing population densities). Lastly, the study suggests that in most cases, only one in 80 million *Tyrannosaurus* would become fossilized, while the chances were likely as high as one in every 16,000 of an individual becoming fossilized in areas that had more dense populations.[^cite_note-nytimes2021-276][^cite_note-Marshall2021-277]

Meiri (2022) questioned the reliability of the estimates, citing uncertainty in metabolic rate, body size, sex and age-specific survival rates, habitat requirements and range size variability as shortcomings Marshall et al. did not take into account.[^cite_note-278] The authors of the original publication replied that while they agree that their reported uncertainties were probably too small, their framework is flexible enough to accommodate uncerainty in physiology, and that their calculations do not depend on short-term changes but rather on long-term averages. Finally, they remark that they did estimate the range of reasonable survivorship curves and that they did include uncertainty in the time of onset of sexual maturity and in the growth curve by incorporating the uncertainty in the maximum body mass.[^cite_note-279]

## Cultural significance

Main article: [Tyrannosaurus in popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosaurus_in_popular_culture)



[King Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Kong) fighting *Tyrannosaurus* in the 1933 film

*Tyrannosaurus* is the most widely known dinosaur in [popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_culture). Science writer [Riley Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riley_Black) states, "In all of prehistory, there is no animal that commands our attention quite like *Tyrannosaurus rex*... Since the time this dinosaur was officially named in 1905, the enormous carnivore has stood as the ultimate dinosaur."[^cite_note-280] Paleontologist David Hone notes the popularity of *Tyrannosaurus* models and skeletal displays in museums and writes that films like *[Jurassic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park_\(film\))* and *[King Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Kong_\(1933_film\))* "would not have been the same without it."[^cite_note-281] *T. rex* is the only dinosaur that is commonly known to the general public by its full scientific name ([binomial name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binomial_nomenclature)) and the scientific abbreviation *T. rex* has also come into wide usage.[^cite_note-brochu2003-10]
